Renormalized Bogoliubov Theory for the Nelson Model
Marco Falconi, Jonas Lampart, Nikolai Leopold, David Mitrouskas

Abstract
. We consider the time evolution of the renormalized Nelson model, which describes N N bosons linearly coupled to a quantized scalar field, in the mean-field limit of many particles N ≫ 1 N\gg 1 with coupling constant proportional to N − 1 / 2 N^{-1/2} . First, we show that initial states exhibiting Bose–Einstein condensation for the particles and approximating a coherent state for the quantum field retain their structure under the many-body time evolution. Concretely, the dynamics of the reduced densities are approximated by solutions of two coupled PDEs, the Schrödinger–Klein–Gordon equations. Second, we construct a renormalized Bogoliubov evolution that describes the quantum fluctuations around the Schrödinger–Klein–Gordon equations. This evolution is used to extend the approximation of the evolved many-body state to the full norm topology. In summary, we provide a comprehensive analysis of the Nelson model that reveals the role of renormalization in the mean-field Bogoliubov theory.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary:

81V73
. Secondary:

81T16
.

1.
Introduction and main results

We study the effective behavior of a large number of bosonic particles in
weak interaction with a quantized scalar field. Microscopically, such a
system is described by the Nelson Hamiltonian. This was first introduced in
1964 in the mathematical physics literature by E. Nelson
[
80
]
, and
provides an example of rigorous renormalization in quantum field theory: The
formal Hamiltonian needs to be corrected by the divergent self-energy of the
particles to obtain a self-adjoint operator and associated unitary dynamics.
Renormalization plays a crucial role not only in (mathematical) physics, it
also led, perhaps unexpectedly, to groundbreaking advances in pure and
applied mathematics, from stochastic and nonlinear partial differential
equations to dynamical systems and geometry (see

[
13
,
17
,
53
,
69
]
for some celebrated
examples). A deeper understanding of renormalization is thus of great
relevance for both mathematics and physics. In this paper, we clarify the
role played by renormalization in the mean-field Bogoliubov theory for the
Nelson model.

What came to be known as Bogoliubov theory was introduced in the 1940s as a
heuristic approach to the analysis of excitations in the condensed Bose gas

[
10
]
. After the successful creation of Bose–Einstein
condensates in laboratory experiments during the 1990s, these ideas have
regained significant interest from the mathematical physics community. This
led to the development of a larger research endeavor aimed at providing a
rigorous justification of Bogoliubov’s approach, starting from the many-body
Schrödinger theory. For the low-energy excitation spectrum of large bosonic
systems, Bogoliubov theory was first justified by Seiringer

[
88
]
and Grech and Seiringer
[
44
]
. Regarding the
time evolution of excitations in many-particle systems, pioneering results
were obtained by Ginibre and Velo
[
39
,
40
]
and Grillakis,
Machedon and Margetis
[
50
,
49
]
. Over the past decade, there has
been substantial progress in developing refined methods for the derivation of
Bogoliubov’s theory and in extending the analysis to cover more singular
interactions, e.g.
[
7
,
66
,
67
,
14
]
and we
refer to Section
1.3
for a detailed overview. In the
present paper, we continue this effort by establishing Bogoliubov’s
approximation for the time evolution in the Nelson model. What sets this
problem qualitatively apart from previous works is the need for
renormalization of the underlying many-body Hamiltonian at finite particle
number and the corresponding Bogoliubov evolution. Our results shed new light
on the behavior of such systems and we hope that they pave the way for
further investigation of the interplay between many-body effects and singular
particle-field interactions. It is worth mentioning that such systems are of
continued relevance in physics. To cite a recent example from condensed
matter theory, the interplay of many-body effects and singular particle-field
interactions is crucial in quantum fluids of light
[
37
,
18
,
34
]
. There, a renormalized Bogoliubov theory of the
condensate-phonon interaction is necessary to explain the properties of
concrete polariton-exciton condensates
[
37
]
.

Now in more detail, we investigate the dynamical evolution of the Nelson
model in a mean-field limit in which the number of particles, denoted by
N
,
becomes large while the coupling to the scalar field is proportional to
1/\sqrt{N}
. Our first result concerns the dynamics of the one-particle and
one-field-mode reduced density matrices. We assume that, at the initial time,
the particles exhibit Bose–Einstein condensation and the quantum field is
approximately in a coherent state. We prove that the time evolution of the
reduced density matrices of such initial states can be described by a
condensate wave function and a classical scalar field that solve a system of
two coupled PDEs, the Schrödinger–Klein–Gordon equations, with errors that
tend to zero as
N\to\infty
. The renormalization on the microscopic level,
interestingly, does not appear in the mean-field equations. This was observed
earlier by Z. Ammari and one of the authors in
[
2
]
, who proved a
similar statement using semiclassical techniques and without quantitative
bounds. In this article we employ different techniques that yield an
explicit rate of convergence for initial states satisfying an energy
condition.

Our second result provides an approximation in norm of the time evolved
many-body state, by a state obtained from the Schrödinger–Klein–Gordon
equations and a quadratic Bogoliubov type evolution modeling the quantum
fluctuations around the mean-field dynamics. On the level of the quantum
fluctuations, it is important to take the renormalization into account and
construct a renormalized Bogoliubov evolution, whose construction may be of
interest on its own.

1.1.
The Nelson Hamiltonian

We consider the massive Nelson model in the mean-field regime. It describes a
system of
N
non-relativistic bosonic particles that are linearly coupled to
a scalar quantum field, whose states are elements of the Hilbert space

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{N}=\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{N}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\otimes\mathcal{F}

where
\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{C}\Omega\oplus\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{n}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is the
bosonic Fock space over
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with vacuum state
\Omega
. The state of the
system evolves according to the Schrödinger equation

(1.2)

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\Psi_{N}(t)=H_{N}\Psi_{N}(t).

Formally, the Nelson Hamiltonian
H_{N}
is given by the expression

(1.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[-\Delta_{j}+N^{-1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dk\,\omega^{-1/2}(k)\left(e^{-ikx_{j}}a^{*}_{k}+e^{ikx_{j}}a_{k}\right)\right]+\text{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega),

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
denote the variables of the particles,
\omega(k)=\sqrt{k^{2}+1}
and
\text{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dk\,\omega(k)a_{k}^{*}a_{k}
is the second
quantization of the multiplication operator
\omega
, describing the energy of the
quantum field. The annihilation and creation operators are defined by the
distribution-valued expressions

(1.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(a_{k}\Psi_{N}\right)^{(n)}(X_{N},K_{n})&=\sqrt{n+1}\Psi_{N}^{(n+1)}(X_{N},k,K_{n}),\\
\left(a_{k}^{*}\Psi_{N}\right)^{(n)}(X_{N},K_{n})&=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta(k-k_{j})\Psi_{N}^{(n-1)}(X_{N},K_{n}\setminus k_{j})\end{split}

with
\Psi^{(n)}_{N}\in\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{N}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\otimes\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{n}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
X_{N}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
,
K_{n}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
. They satisfy the
canonical commutation relations

(1.5)

\displaystyle[a_{k},a^{*}_{l}]

\displaystyle=\delta(k-l),\quad[a_{k},a_{l}]=[a^{*}_{k},a^{*}_{l}]=0.

This definition of
H_{N}
is only formal, since no domain has been specified.
The quadratic form associated to the expression is ill defined on the
form-domain of the non-interacting Hamiltonian, and while it may be defined
on more regular states, this makes it unbounded from below and not closable.
However, this problem can be remedied by renormalization
[
80
]
:
Denote by
H_{N}^{\Lambda}
the version of (
1.3
) with
\omega^{-1/2}
replaced by
\omega^{-1/2}\mathds{1}_{|k|\leq\Lambda}
in the interaction, then there exists a diverging family of
numbers
E^{\Lambda}
and a self-adjoint operator
H_{N}
,
D(H_{N})
so that

(1.6)

e^{-itH_{N}}=\slim_{\Lambda\to\infty}e^{-itH_{N}^{\Lambda}}e^{-itE^{\Lambda}}.

We take this as the definition of the Nelson Hamiltonian
H_{N}
, and remark
that, due to the coupling constant
N^{-1/2}
, the numbers
E^{\Lambda}
can be
chosen independent of
N
. The operator
H_{N}
can be characterized further
by applying a dressing transformation, see
[
80
,
46
]
and
Lemma
3.1
, or by an alternative approach related to
boundary conditions
[
58
]
. It is important to note the effect of
the renormalization on the domain of
H_{N}
. While the operators
H_{N}^{\Lambda}

all share the domain of the free Hamiltonian, it holds that
D(H_{N}^{1/2})\cap H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})\otimes\mathcal{F}=\{0\}
, i.e., even the form domain of
H_{N}
is
completely different from that of the free Hamiltonian
[
46
,
58
]
.

An important role in our analysis is played by a unitary transformation,
usually called the dressing transformation, that relates the renormalized
Nelson Hamiltonian to an operator whose quadratic form is more explicit and,
importantly, comparable to the one of the free Hamiltonian. Although our
main results below can be stated without any reference to this
transformation, it is crucial in their proofs.

1.2.
Main results

In this section we state our results on the approximation for the Nelson time
evolution, first on the level of reduced densities by the mean-field
equations, and then in norm by a renormalized Bogoliubov evolution.

Mean-field approximation.

We are interested in the evolution of many-body states in which the particles
form a Bose–Einstein condensate and the field is approximately in a coherent
state. To be more precise, let us define the unitary Weyl operator

(1.7)

\displaystyle W(f)=\exp\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dk\,\big(f(k)a^{*}_{k}-\overline{f(k)}a_{k}\big)\right).

The initial states we have in mind are of the form

(1.8)

\displaystyle\Psi_{N}\approx u^{\otimes N}\otimes W(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Omega

with
\Omega
being the vacuum in
\mathcal{F}
and
u,\alpha\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. We will show that this product-like structure is
preserved during the time evolution and that

(1.9)

\displaystyle e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N}\approx u_{t}^{\otimes N}\otimes W(\sqrt{N}\alpha_{t})\Omega,

where
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
solve
the Schrödinger–Klein–Gordon (SKG) equations

(1.10)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} i\partial_{t}u_{t}(x)&\,=\,\big(-\Delta+\phi_{\alpha_{t}}(x)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big\langle u_{t},\phi_{\alpha_{t}}u_{t}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\big)u_{t}(x)\\[4.2679pt]
i\partial_{t}\alpha_{t}(k)&\,=\,\omega(k)\alpha_{t}(k)+\big\langle u_{t},G_{(\cdot)}(k)u_{t}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\\[4.2679pt]
\end{aligned}\end{cases}

where

(1.11)

\displaystyle G_{x}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega(k)}}e^{-ikx}\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{\alpha}(x)=2\mathrm{Re}\big\langle G_{x},\alpha\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

These equations are the Hamiltonian equations of the energy

(1.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\big\langle u,\left(-\Delta+\phi_{\alpha}\right)u\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\big\langle\alpha,\omega\alpha\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

We denote the flow of solutions to the SKG equations by

\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)=(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
, that is
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
solves
(
1.10
) with initial conditions

(u_{t},\alpha_{t})|_{t=0}=(u,\alpha)
. In our main results, we will use that

\mathfrak{s}
is well defined on
H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}

where
H^{3}
denotes the
L^{2}
-Sobolev space and
\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
is the
weighted space with norm
\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}}=\|\omega^{5/2}\alpha\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}
. Moreover, the SKG flow conserves the energy

\mathcal{E}
and the
L^{2}
-norm of
u_{t}
. These statements follow from the
more general well-posedness result summarized in Proposition
2.2
.

Our first result states that the one-particle reduced density matrices of a
state
e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N}
are close to those of the product
state (
1.9
) if
this holds at the initial time and if the energy expectation per particle of

\Psi_{N}
is close to the initial mean-field energy. A convenient measure for
the convergence of reduced densities is given by the functional

(1.13)

\displaystyle\beta\left[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)\right]

\displaystyle=\big\langle\Psi_{N},(q_{u})_{1}\Psi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}+N^{-1}\|\mathcal{N}_{a}^{1/2}W^{*}(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Psi_{N}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{N}},

where
(q_{u})_{1}
denotes the orthogonal projection
q_{u}=\mathds{1}-|u\rangle\langle u|
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
acting on the first particle’s variable

x_{1}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{a}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dk\,a_{k}^{*}a_{k}
is the number
operator on
\mathcal{F}
. The functional
\beta
counts the number of
particles in states orthogonal to
u
and the number of field modes outside
of the coherent state
W(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Omega
, both relative to the total
number
N
of particles. In particular,
\beta[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)]=0

for states of the product form (
1.8
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1
and let
\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)=(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
denote the solution of (
1.10
) with initial data
(u,\alpha)
. Then, there exists a
constant
C>0
such that for all
N\geq 1
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Psi_{N}\in D(H_{N}^{1/2})
with
\|\Psi_{N}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}=1
,

\beta\left[e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)\right]\leq e^{CR(t)}\Big(\big|N^{-1}\big\langle\Psi_{N},H_{N}\Psi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}-\mathcal{E}(u,\alpha)\big|\\
+\max_{j=1,2}\big(\beta\left[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)\right]+N^{-1}\big)^{1/j}\Big)

with
R(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}(\|u_{s}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{10}+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}}^{10})ds
, and
\mathcal{E}
defined by
(
1.12
).

The proof of the theorem is given in Section
3.4
. We note that
R(t)
does not grow faster than polynomially in
time by Proposition
2.2
. Initial states of interest
are of course those for which the right-hand side is small. It is important
to mention that this is not the case if
\Psi_{N}
is exactly of the product
form (
1.8
). Indeed, due to the singular nature
of the Nelson Hamiltonian such states are not in the form domain of
H_{N}
,
see
[
46
,
58
]
. Next, we provide an example of initial states in
the form domain of the Nelson Hamiltonian that are close to product
states. To this end, we modify the large momenta by means of a dressing
transformation.

Proposition 1.2
.

For
(u,\alpha)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
K\geq 0
, let
B_{K,x}=(k^{2}+\omega)^{-1}G_{K,x}\mathds{1}_{|k|\geq K}
and define

\Psi_{N,K}:=\prod_{j=1}^{N}W^{*}(N^{-1/2}B_{K,x_{j}})\big(u^{\otimes N}\otimes W(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Omega\big).

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
(u,\alpha)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{1}
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1
,
K>0
and
N\geq 1

\displaystyle\beta[\Psi_{N,K},(u,\alpha)]

\displaystyle\leq CK^{-1}\left(1+\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\right)

\displaystyle\big|N^{-1}\big\langle\Psi_{N,K},H_{N}\Psi_{N,K}\big\rangle-\mathcal{E}(u,\alpha)\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(K^{-1}+\tfrac{1+\ln K}{N}\right)\big(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{1}}^{2}\big).

The proof is given in Appendix
A
. Note that the
dressing transformation used above converges strongly to the identity as

K\to\infty
, and thus
\Psi_{N,K}
for large
K
is close to a product
state also in the norm topology. For initial states
\Psi_{K,N}
with
K=N
,
Theorem
1.1
simplifies to the
following form.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1
and let
\Psi_{N,N}
be the state
defined in Proposition
1.2

for
K=N
. Then, there exist constants
C>0
,
\delta>0
so that

\displaystyle\beta\left[e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N,N},\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)\right]\leq e^{C(1+|t|^{\delta})}N^{-1/2}.

Proof.

The corollary is a direct consequence of Theorem
1.1
, Proposition
1.2
and the bound on the solutions to the SKG equations of
Proposition
2.2
.
∎

Remark 1.1

(Convergence of reduced densities)
.

We briefly explain how Theorem
1.1

relates to the approximation of reduced densities. To this end, recall the
definition of the reduced one-particle density matrix for the bosons,

(1.14)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Psi_{N}}^{(1,0)}=N\,\text{Tr}_{2,\ldots,N}\otimes\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{F}}\big(|\Psi_{N}\rangle\langle\Psi_{N}|\big)

where
\text{Tr}_{2,\ldots,N}
is the partial trace w.r.t.
(x_{2},\ldots,x_{N})
,
and the definition of the reduced density of the field, given in terms of
its integral kernel

(1.15)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Psi_{N}}^{(0,1)}(k,l)=\big\langle\Psi_{N},a_{l}^{*}a_{k}\Psi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}.

Their distance, measured in trace norm, to the density operators obtained
from the solutions of the SKG equations can be controlled by
\beta
from
(
1.13
) via the inequalities

[
63
, Lem. VII.2]

(1.16a)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\big|\gamma_{\Psi_{N}}^{(1,0)}-N|u\rangle\langle u|\big|

\displaystyle\leq N\sqrt{8\big\langle\Psi_{N},(q_{u})_{1}\Psi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}},

(1.16b)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\big|\gamma_{\Psi_{N}}^{(0,1)}-N|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|\big|

\displaystyle\leq 3\|\mathcal{N}_{a}^{1/2}W^{*}(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Psi_{N}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}

\displaystyle\qquad+6\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|\mathcal{N}_{a}^{1/2}W^{*}(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Psi_{N}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}.

For
\Psi_{N}(t)=e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N}
and
(u_{t}
,
\alpha_{t})
, we
consequently get a bound on the difference of reduced densities in terms of
the right-hand side of the bound in Theorem
1.1
. That is, for suitable initial states (such as those
of Corollary
1.3
) the average boson behaves like
u_{t}
and
there are on average
N
field modes behaving like
\alpha_{t}
.

Bogoliubov approximation.
In the following we define the renormalized Nelson–Bogoliubov evolution and explain its role in approximating the fluctuations in the Nelson dynamics.
We gather the quantum fluctuations around the condensate with wave function

u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and the coherent state associated with the field

\sqrt{N}\alpha\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
in an element
\chi\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
that is orthogonal to
u
in every variable

x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
. That is,

(1.17)

\chi\in\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{k}\{u\}^{\perp}\otimes\mathcal{F}=:\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}.

For any
\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}
one obtains such a
\chi:=X_{u,\alpha}\Psi_{N}
using a variant of the excitation map introduced in
[
67
]
,
see Section
2.1
for details. To describe the inverse
of this map, let
\chi^{(k)}
denote the component of
\chi
in the
k
-th
summand of (
1.17
). If
\chi^{(k)}=0
for
k>N
,
we can reconstruct
\Psi_{N}
as

(1.18)

\Psi_{N}=W(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\sum_{k=0}^{N}u^{\otimes N-k}\otimes_{\rm s}\chi^{(k)}=X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi,

where the symmetric tensor product has to be understood as the tensor product
of the subspaces of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\mathrm{span}(u)
and
\{u\}^{\perp}
, so
that each summand yields an element of
\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{N}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\otimes\mathcal{F}
, on which the Weyl operator
W(\sqrt{N}\alpha)
acts in
the second tensor factor.
1
1

1

More precisely, we set
u^{\otimes N-k}\otimes_{\rm s}\chi^{(k)}:=P_{\rm sym}^{N}(u^{\otimes N-k}\otimes\chi^{(k)})
, where the tensor product is taken w.r.t. the spaces

\text{Span}(u)^{\otimes N-k}
and
(\{u\}^{\perp})^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathcal{F}
and where
P^{N}_{\rm sym}
is the orthogonal projection onto the
symmetric subspace of
\bigotimes^{N}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, while it acts as the
identity on
\mathcal{F}
.
Note that the product state (
1.8
) would correspond to
\chi=\Omega\otimes\Omega
.

Now let
\Psi_{N}(t)
and
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
be solutions of
(
1.2
) and (
1.10
) with suitable initial conditions
\Psi_{N}
and

(u,\alpha)
, and consider the fluctuation vector
\chi(t)
satisfying

\Psi_{N}(t)=X_{u_{t},\alpha_{t}}^{*}\chi(t)
. This vector is an element of

\mathcal{F}_{\perp u_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, which we can naturally identify with a
subspace of the double Fock space

(1.19)

\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}\cong\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{n}\big(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\big).

We will denote the creation and annihilation operator on the first factor in

\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, associated with excitations of the bosons, by
b^{*}
and

b
.

The dynamics of the fluctuations
\chi(t)
will be approximated by a time
dependent Bogoliubov transformation. Roughly speaking, Bogoliubov
transformations on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
are unitary maps that are determined (up
to a phase) by a map on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. This
property makes Bogoliubov transformations much simpler in terms of
complexity. To be more precise, define the joint creation operator of the
excitations and the field by

(1.20)

c^{*}(f\oplus g)=b^{*}(f)+a^{*}(g),

and the annihilation operator as its adjoint. A Bogoliubov transformation on

\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
is a unitary map
\mathbb{U}
with the
property that

(1.21)

\mathbb{U}^{*}c^{*}(f\oplus g)\mathbb{U}=c^{*}(\mathfrak{u}(f\oplus g))+c(\mathfrak{v}(\overline{f\oplus g}))

for some bounded linear maps
\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In other words, conjugation of

a^{*}
,
b^{*}
by
\mathbb{U}
maps these to linear combinations of
a,a^{*},b,b^{*}
with modified arguments. For a more detailed introduction of Bogoliubov
transformations and related concepts, we refer to
[
89
]
and

[
12
, Sec. 4]
.

The generators of Bogoliubov transformations are (formally) Hamiltonians
quadratic in the creation and annihilation operators,
a,a^{*},b,b^{*}
. For
the Nelson model with ultraviolet cutoff, such a quadratic generator can be
obtained from the full Hamiltonian
[
33
]
following the
approximation ideas of Bogoliubov
[
10
]
. The
Nelson–Bogoliubov Hamiltonian with cutoff
\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
is
defined by

(1.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dx\,b^{*}_{x}h_{\alpha_{t}}b_{x}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dk\,\omega(k)a^{*}_{k}a_{k}

\displaystyle\,+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}dxdk\Big(\big(q_{u_{t}}G^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)u_{t}\big)(x)\,a^{*}_{k}b_{x}^{*}+\big(q_{u_{t}}\overline{G^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)}u_{t}\big)(x)\,a_{k}b_{x}^{*}\Big)+\text{h.c.},

where
G_{x}^{\Lambda}(k)=G_{x}(k)\mathds{1}_{\left|k\right|\leq\Lambda}
and

h_{\alpha_{t}}=-\Delta+\phi_{\alpha_{t}}-\frac{1}{2}\big\langle u_{t},\phi_{\alpha_{t}}u_{t}\big\rangle
with
G_{x}(k)
and
\phi_{\alpha_{t}}
given by
(
1.11
), and where
h.c.
denotes the hermitian
conjugate of the preceding term. Let
\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}(t)
be the unique
unitary propagator (with initial time
t=0
) on the double Fock space

\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
associated with
\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(t)
. For a
discussion of its existence, we refer to
[
33
, Thm. 4.1]
or
Proposition
5.2
below with
\theta=0
. Our next result
states the existence of a renormalized Nelson–Bogoliubov time evolution in
the limit
\Lambda\to\infty
. We note that the numbers
E^{\Lambda}
below,
as given explicitly in Proposition
5.3
, have the same
asymptotic behavior as those in the renormalization of the Nelson
Hamiltonian (
1.6
).

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1
and let

(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
denote the solution of (
1.10
) with initial datum
(u,\alpha)
. There exists a family

E^{\Lambda}
(with
E^{\Lambda}\to\infty
as
\Lambda\to\infty
) such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}(t):=\slim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}(t)e^{-itE^{\Lambda}}

exists for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{U}(t)
has the
following properties:

(i)

\mathbb{U}(t)
is unitary and strongly continuous
in
t
,

(ii)

\mathbb{U}(t)(\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F})\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\perp u_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
,

(iii)

\mathbb{U}(t)
is a Bogoliubov transformation on

\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
.

Our second main result is a norm approximation of the dynamics generated by
the Nelson Hamiltonian. It states that the fluctuations around the condensate
wave function
u_{t}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and the coherent state associated
with the field mode
\sqrt{N}\alpha_{t}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
are effectively
described by the renormalized Nelson–Bogoliubov evolution introduced in
Theorem
1.4
. Together with the fact that
\mathbb{U}(t)
is a Bogoliubov transformation, this implies an approximation of the
Nelson evolution in terms of a transformation of the form
(
1.21
).

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1
and
let
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
denote the solution to
(
1.10
) for initial data
(u,\alpha)
.
There exists a quadratic form
\delta\geq 1
whose domain is dense in

\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
and a constant
C>0
so that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
N\geq 1
and
\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
with
\|\chi\|_{\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}}=1
, we have for

\Psi_{N}=X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi
given by (
1.18
)

\Big\|e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N}-W(\sqrt{N}\alpha_{t})\sum_{k=0}^{N}u_{t}^{\otimes N-k}\otimes_{\rm s}(\mathbb{U}(t)\chi)^{(k)}\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}\\
\leq e^{CR(t)}\delta(\chi)^{1/2}\frac{\sqrt{\ln N}}{N^{1/4}},

where
R(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}(\|u_{s}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{10}+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}}^{10})ds
.

This theorem is proved in Section
4.3
.

Remark 1.2
.

The quadratic form
\delta
is constructed explicitly using a unitary
Bogoliubov transformation
\mathbb{W}
that implements a dressing on the
level of the fluctuation vector
\chi
. With this transformation, which is
introduced in Proposition
4.3
as
\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(1)
,
\delta
is given by

(1.23)

\displaystyle\delta(\chi)=\|\chi\|^{2}_{\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}}+\big\langle\mathbb{W}\chi,(\mathcal{N}^{3}+\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{b}(-\Delta)+\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega))\mathbb{W}\chi\big\rangle_{\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}}\,,

i.e., it measures the expectation of the third moment of the number of
excitations and the energy of the excitations and the field after dressing
with
\mathbb{W}
. In Proposition
4.3
, we also
show that
\mathbb{W}
preserves the domain of
\mathcal{N}^{3/2}
. This is
relevant, as it implies that the norm of the initial state
\Psi_{N}
in
(
1.5
) approaches one as
N\to\infty
. More precisely,
it implies that
\|X^{*}_{u,\alpha}\chi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}\geq\|\chi\|_{\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}}-CN^{-3}\delta(\chi)
for some
C>0
. On the other hand,
let us note that we do not expect that
\mathbb{W}
preserves the norm of

\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{b}(-\Delta)
.

The following corollary extends the norm approximation of
Theorem
1.5
to all initial states with finite number
of excitations, in the sense that
X_{u,\alpha}\Psi_{N}
has a well-defined
limit as
N\to\infty
.

Corollary 1.6
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1
and let
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
denote the
solution to (
1.10
) for
initial data
(u,\alpha)
. Let
X_{u_{t},\alpha_{t}}^{*}
be the adjoint of
the excitation map, given by (
1.18
). Let
\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
with
\|\chi\|_{\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}}=1
and let
\Psi_{N}
,
N\geq 1
, be such that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\|\Psi_{N}-X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}=0
. Then, for all
T>0

\lim_{N\to\infty}\|e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N}-X_{u_{t},\alpha_{t}}^{*}\mathbb{U}(t)\chi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}=0

uniformly for
|t|\leq T
.

Proof.

This follows from the density of the domain of the quadratic form
\delta

by an approximation argument, using that
e^{-itH_{N}}
,
\mathbb{U}(t)

and
X_{u,\alpha}
are isometries. To be precise, let
\varepsilon>0
and
choose
N
large enough so that
\Psi_{N}-X^{*}_{u,\alpha}\chi
has norm less
than
\varepsilon
.
Then choose
\chi_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
in the domain
of the form
\delta
with
\|\chi-\chi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}}<\varepsilon
. The difference of
e^{-itH_{N}}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi_{\varepsilon}
and
X_{u_{t},\alpha_{t}}^{*}\mathbb{U}(t)\chi_{\varepsilon}
converges to zero by
Theorem
1.5
, so its norm is smaller than

\varepsilon
for
N
sufficiently large. Using unitarity of
e^{-itH_{N}}
,
\mathbb{U}(t)
, and
\|X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\|=1
, this implies that

(1.24)

\displaystyle\|e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N}-X_{u_{t},\alpha_{t}}^{*}\mathbb{U}(t)\chi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}<4\varepsilon,

which proves the claim.
∎

1.3.
Comparison with the literature

The broader subject of this work, the justification of the time-dependent
mean-field and Bogoliubov approximations, has been addressed extensively in
the literature, mainly in the context of the Bose gas with two-body pair
interaction. The situation of particles coupled to a quantum field has been
explored to a much lesser extent. Below we give a brief overview of the
literature on this topic and other works related to this study.

The first works on the mean-field approximation of reduced densities for the
many-body Bose gas with two-body interaction date back to the 1970s and 1980s
by Hepp, Ginibre, Velo, and Spohn
[
54
,
40
,
39
,
90
]
. The
question was revived in the early 2000s
[
4
,
31
]
and within the
next years, new techniques were developed to obtain explicit rates of
convergence
[
19
,
84
,
87
]
and to cover more singular two-body
potentials, in particular those converging to a Dirac-delta potential

[
29
,
30
,
56
,
85
,
5
]
. Since then, this topic
continues to be actively studied and we recommend
[
79
,
6
,
43
]
for a comprehensive survey of recent works. Fluctuations
around the time-dependent mean-field equations were considered first in

[
40
,
39
,
50
,
49
]
. Since
[
66
,
67
]
,
this subject has gained increased interest which led to further extensions
and refinements in the derivation of the Bogoliubov approximation, see
e.g.
[
27
,
73
,
72
,
14
,
74
,
9
,
71
,
83
,
20
,
76
,
57
,
75
,
48
]
. Higher-order corrections to Bogoliubov theory have
been obtained in
[
11
,
12
,
42
,
41
]
. Let us note that Bogoliubov theory plays a crucial
role also in the description of the excitation spectrum of large bosonic
systems. While this has been extensively studied for bosons with two-body
potentials
[
78
,
77
,
52
,
44
,
88
,
7
,
28
,
8
,
67
,
86
]
, we are not aware of any
results concerning the spectral properties of many bosons coupled to a
quantum field.

The derivation of the SKG equations starting from the renormalized Nelson
model, in the same limit as considered in this work, has been addressed
previously by
[
2
]
. Using techniques from semiclassical theory, the
authors demonstrate that the Wigner measure associated with the many-body
dynamics evolves in the limit
N\to\infty
in accordance with the
push-forward of a Wigner measure under the SKG flow. Since convergence of the
Wigner measure implies weak-
\ast
convergence for the reduced densities,
this statement is comparable to Theorem
1.1
of the present work. Unlike the approach taken in
[
2
]
,
which provides a limit result without explicit error estimates, our method
allows us to determine an explicit rate of convergence for the reduced
densities. On the other hand, the results in
[
2
]
apply to a wider
class of initial states. Regarding our second result, the construction of the
renormalized Nelson–Bogoliubov Hamiltonian and the norm approximation, we
are not aware of any prior work that has addressed this problem.

More results have been obtained for models with regular particle-field
interactions (i.e., without need for renormalization): For the regularized
Nelson model with ultraviolet cutoff, derivations of the corresponding
mean-field dynamics were obtained in
[
1
,
32
,
63
]
and the
validity of the Bogoliubov approximation as well as higher-order corrections
was established in
[
33
]
. In addition, the regularized Nelson
model was studied also in a many-fermion limit that is closely linked to a
semiclassical limit
[
62
]
. Other particle-field systems, such as the
Fröhlich model and the Pauli–Fierz Hamiltonian, have been studied in the
scaling regime of the present article too, see
[
61
,
64
]
for the
mean-field approximation and
[
59
]
for an approximation of the
Fröhlich dynamics in norm. The dressed Nelson Hamiltonan, which will play a
crucial role in our analysis (see Section
1.4
), has similar
regularity properties to the Fröhlich Hamiltonian, as both are given in
terms of perturbations of the non-interacting quadratic form. However, the
dressed Nelson Hamiltonian has a more complicated structure than the
Fröhlich Hamiltonian since it is not linear in creation and annihilation
operators. This makes the analysis of the time evolution more involved
already on the level of the dressed Hamiltonian.

Within the broader scope of deriving effective equations from particle-field
models, it is worth noting the following works. The subject of

[
22
,
23
,
24
,
38
]
is a partially classical limit of a
class of models (covering the regularized Nelson, and the Fröhlich and
Pauli–Fierz models), where a fixed number of particles is weakly coupled to
a quantum scalar field with high occupation number. For the Fröhlich
Hamiltonian specifically, the time evolution has been actively studied also
in the strong coupling regime
[
35
,
36
,
45
,
60
,
65
,
70
]
. While the resulting effective equations are of similar
form as the SKG equations, the strong coupling limit is accompanied by a
separation of time scales between the particle and the field, a feature that
is absent in the mean-field limit. The papers
[
25
,
55
,
91
]
focus on the derivation of effective pair particle
potentials arising from the particle-field interaction, in suitable
weak-coupling and adiabatic limits.

Finally, for an overview of results on the renormalized Nelson model not
directly linked to the derivation of effective equations, we refer to the
discussion of
[
68
]
.

1.4.
Outline of the proofs

General idea.
The Hamiltonian expressed formally in (
1.3
) can be represented in terms of an operator

H_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}
with more regular and explicitly given quadratic form,
conjugated with a unitary dressing transformation
W^{\mathrm{D}}
, see
Lemma
3.1
. By unitarity of
W^{\rm D}
, this allows
us to relate the Nelson dynamics to the dynamics generated by the dressed
Hamiltonian via

(1.25)

\displaystyle e^{-itH_{N}}=(W^{\rm D})^{*}e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm{D}}}W^{\rm{D}}.

The general strategy of the proof is to analyze the mean-field and norm
approximations of the dressed time evolution
e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm{D}}}
, and
then connect the corresponding dressed mean-field and Bogoliubov evolutions
to the original (undressed) ones. To accomplish this, we will introduce
approximations of the dressing transformation that relate the dressed and
undressed effective evolutions, in analogy to the relation shown in
(
1.25
). Denoting the dressed mean-field
flow and the dressed Bogoliubov evolution as
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]
and

\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)
, respectively, and the approximations of the dressing
transformation by
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathbb{W}
, then the connection between
the effective evolutions can be expressed as

(1.26)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}[t]=\mathfrak{D}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D},\qquad\mathbb{U}(t)=\mathbb{W}^{*}\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)\mathbb{W}.

To determine
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathbb{W}
, we view
W^{\rm D}=W^{\rm D}(1)

as the special case of a quantum evolution operator
W^{\rm D}(\theta)
with
“time”
\theta
and examine its mean-field and Bogoliubov
approximations. The motivation for this stems from the observation that

W^{\rm D}(\theta)
is a unitary group that is generated by a field operator
resembling the interaction term in (
1.3
), but with a square-integrable form factor replacing

\omega^{-1/2}
. While the mean-field flow approximation
\mathfrak{D}[\theta]
has been proposed and studied previously in
[
2
]
, we
extend this idea to the level of the Bogoliubov approximation. One of the
difficulties that arises in this context is that the effective dressing

\mathbb{W}(\theta)
will be generated by a non-autonomous equation.

In the proof of Theorem
1.4
, we establish an identity
similar to (
1.26
) but for
\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}(t)e^{-itE_{\Lambda}}
and with
\Lambda
-dependent versions of

\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)
, and then use that the cutoff can be
removed for the conjugated dressed evolution.

Mean-field approximation.
In order to derive
Theorem
1.1
, we consider the dressed
dynamics
e^{-itH_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}}
applied to the dressed initial state

W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N}
, and compare it with the corresponding mean-field equations
introduced in (
5.2
), whose flow is
denoted by
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]
. This is the content of Theorem

3.2
, which gives an
analogous statement to Theorem
1.1

but for the dressed evolutions. The proof of
Theorem
3.2
relies
on the use of the excitation map and estimates on the generator of the
fluctuation dynamics. To relate the two theorems, i.e., to pass from the
approximation of
e^{-itH_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}}
to that of
e^{-itH_{N}}
, we
then expand on the idea that the dressing
W^{\rm D}
itself can be
approximated by the mean-field dressing transformation
\mathfrak{D}
. This is
the subject of Lemmas
3.5
and

3.6
, with the latter providing the reason for the
required energy condition in Theorem
1.1
. Since
\mathfrak{D}
interpolates between the dressed and the
undressed mean-field evolutions, i.e.
\mathfrak{s}[t]=\mathfrak{D}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}
, this allows us to translate
the approximation result of the dressed dynamics to the desired result on the
undressed ones. The explained strategy is summarized in the commutative
diagram of Figure
1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{a}}{\Psi_{N}}{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{D}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\to\infty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\to\infty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mspace{25mu}N\to\infty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W^{\rm D})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mspace{70mu}N\to\infty}

Figure 1.
Diagram schematizing Theorem
1.1
and its relation with the dressed counterpart,
Theorem
3.2
. Each microscopic state on the front face is close to the
associated mean-field state in the limit
N\to\infty
(lying on the
face beyond), as measured by the functional
\beta
. The arrows are
kept unlabeled if the associated map is obvious.

Since the proof of Theorem
1.1
is
based on Theorem
3.2
for the dressed Hamiltonian and this diagram, the natural initial
condition would be a many-body state of the form

(1.27)

\displaystyle\Psi_{N}=(W^{\rm D})^{*}(u^{\rm D})^{\otimes N}\otimes W(\sqrt{N}\alpha^{\rm D})\Omega.

However, such states are not close to product states of the
form (
1.8
) when measured by the full Hilbert
space norm (by Lemma
3.5
, they are
close to such states when measured by
\beta)
. In Corollary

1.3
we show that Theorem
1.1
also applies to states that are close to product states. These
are obtained from Proposition
1.2
, which shows that it is sufficient to apply the dressing to momenta
larger than
K=N
to solve the problem that exact product
states (
1.8
) are not in the form domain of the
Nelson Hamiltonian.

Bogoliubov approximation.
The general strategy of the norm
approximation resembles the one of the mean-field approximation. That is, we
start again from the analysis of the dressed dynamics, where in analogy to
the discussion preceding Theorem
1.5
, we now examine
the fluctuation vector
\chi^{\rm D}(t)=X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)}e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi
associated
with the dressed mean-field flow and compare it with the effective evolution

\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)\chi
. Here,
\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)
is obtained from
the Bogoliubov approximation of the dressed dynamics. The statement analogous
to Theorem
1.5
for the dressed dynamics is given in
Theorem
4.2
, whose proof is based
on estimates on the difference of the generator of the dressed fluctuations
and the quadratic generator of
\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)
. To establish a
connection of this result to the one for the undressed dynamics, we use
(
1.25
) together with a norm approximation
for the dressing transformation
W^{\mathrm{D}}
. To this end, we elevate the
mean-field approximation
\mathfrak{D}
to the level of the fluctuations by
implementing a Bogoliubov transformation
\mathbb{W}
. For the definition of

\mathbb{W}
, we need to introduce a non-autonomous flow of Bogoliubov
transformations on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, which is the content of Proposition

4.3
. Lemma
4.5
then
demonstrates that
\mathbb{W}
indeed offers a norm approximation of
W^{\rm D}
, in the sense that
X_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}W^{\rm D}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi\approx\mathbb{W}\chi
as
N\to\infty
for suitable

\chi\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
. As a final step, we argue that
\mathbb{W}

interpolates between the dressed and the undressed Bogoliubov evolutions,
i.e. that
\mathbb{U}(t)=\mathbb{W}^{*}\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)\mathbb{W}
, with
the precise version of this identity given in (
5.15
).

Altogether, the argument for the norm approximation is based on the following
sequence of identities and approximations (which amount to the arrows in a
diagram similar to Figure
1
)

\displaystyle e^{-itH_{N}}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi

\displaystyle\ \ \overset{\eqref{eq:relation:dressed:undressed:into}}{=}\ \ (W^{\rm D})^{*}e^{-itH_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}}W^{\rm D}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi

\displaystyle\ \ \overset{{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{blank}}}{\approx}\ \ (W^{\rm D})^{*}e^{-itH_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}}X^{*}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}\mathbb{W}\chi

\displaystyle\ \ \overset{{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{blank}}}{\approx}\ \ (W^{\rm D})^{*}X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)\mathbb{W}\chi

\displaystyle\ \ \overset{\eqref{eq:effective:flows:intro}}{=}\ \ (W^{\rm D})^{*}X_{\mathfrak{D}\circ\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)\mathbb{W}\chi

(1.28)

\displaystyle\ \ \overset{{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{blank}}}{\approx}\ \ X_{\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{W}^{*}\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}(t)\mathbb{W}\chi\ \ \overset{\eqref{eq:effective:flows:intro}}{=}\ \ X_{\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{U}(t)\chi.

Renormalization.
For the purpose of the norm
approximation, we could take (
1.26
) as the
definition of
\mathbb{U}(t)
in Theorem
1.5
.
However, we also want to elucidate the relation of this evolution to the one
that can be formally derived by applying the quadratic Bogoliubov
approximation to (
1.3
). This
relation is given by Theorem
1.4
. To this end, one
needs to introduce an ultraviolet cutoff
\Lambda
, since it is not clear
that the Nelson–Bogoliubov Hamiltonian (
1.22
) defines a
self-adjoint operator for
\Lambda=\infty
. With a cutoff
\Lambda
, one
might expect that the Bogoliubov approximation of the Nelson dynamics is
given exactly by the Bogoliubov approximation of the dressed Hamiltonian,
conjugated with the approximation of the dressing. However, the Bogoliubov
evolution is fixed only up to a phase, so the identity may only hold for an
appropriate choice of such a phase. This is the content of
Proposition
5.3
, where we show that the correct choice of
phase
e^{-itE^{\Lambda}}
is such that
E^{\Lambda}\to\infty
as

\Lambda\to\infty
. The phase serves to renormalize the Bogoliubov
evolution, which is in complete analogy to the renormalization of
H_{N}
, as
stated in (
1.6
).

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Fock spaces and excitation map

We recall the Fock space
\mathcal{F}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{k}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and define the (truncated) Fock spaces for the excitations
of the particles,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{\perp u}=\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{k}\{u\}^{\perp},\quad\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}^{\leq N}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{N}\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{\perp u},\quad\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{\perp u}

for
\{u\}^{\perp}=\{\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\langle\varphi,u\rangle=0\}
. The relevant double Fock spaces for the Nelson model are

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}^{\leq N}\otimes\mathcal{F},\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}\quad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F},

where the first factor always refers to the excitations of the
N
bosonic
particles, while the second factor describes the excitations of the quantum
field. If the context is not unambiguous, we shall write
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{b}
for the
particles and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{a}
for the quantum field. In the order of
(
2.2
), we refer to the Fock spaces as truncated
excitation Fock space, excitation Fock space and double Fock
space. Moreover, we denote by
b_{x}
,
b_{x}^{*}
,
\mathcal{N}_{b}
and
a_{k}
,

a_{k}^{*}
,
\mathcal{N}_{a}
the annihilation, creation and number operators on

\mathcal{F}_{b}
and
\mathcal{F}_{a}
, respectively. For
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
let

(2.3a)

\displaystyle a(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dk\,\overline{f(k)}a_{k},\quad a^{*}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dk\,f(k)a_{k}^{*},

(2.3b)

\displaystyle b(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dx\,\overline{f(x)}b_{x},\quad b^{*}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dx\,f(x)b_{x}^{*}

denote the bosonic annihilation and creation operators. For self-adjoint

T
,
D(T)
on
L^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
we denote by
\mathrm{d}\Gamma(T)
the
self-adjoint second quantization of
T
on
\mathcal{F}
. Depending on which factor
of
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
this acts, we write

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{a}(T)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}dxdy\,T(k,l)a_{k}^{*}a_{l}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{b}(T)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}dxdy\,T(x,y)b_{x}^{*}b_{y},

where
T(\cdot,\cdot)
is the Schwarz kernel of
T
. With this, we introduce
the notation

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{b}+\mathcal{N}_{a},\qquad\mathbb{T}=\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{a}\left(-\Delta\right)+\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega).

We define the field operator by

(2.6)

\hat{\Phi}(f)=a(f)+a^{*}(f).

Using this definition, the Weyl operator introduced in (
1.7
) can be expressed as

(2.7)

W(f)=e^{-i\hat{\Phi}(if)}.

It satisfies

(2.8)

\displaystyle W^{-1}(f)=W(-f),\quad W(f)W(g)=W(f+g)e^{-i\mathrm{Im}\langle f,g\rangle},

as well as the shift property

(2.9)

\displaystyle W^{*}(f)a_{k}W(f)=a_{k}+f(k).

As an important tool in our analysis, we introduce a variant of the

excitation map
introduced in
[
66
,
67
]
. In the
context of the Nelson model, the excitation map factors out a condensate with
wave function
u
and a coherent state with field mode
\sqrt{N}\alpha
. For

u,\alpha\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
, it is defined as
the map

(2.10)

X_{u,\alpha}:\mathcal{H}_{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}

with
\Psi_{N}\mapsto(\chi^{(k)})_{k=0}^{N}
given by

(2.11)

\displaystyle\chi^{(k)}=\binom{N}{k}^{1/2}\prod_{i=1}^{k}(q_{u})_{i}\big\langle u^{\otimes(N-k)},W^{*}(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Psi_{N}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3(N-k)})}\;\in\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}^{(k)}\otimes\mathcal{F},

where
(q_{u})_{i}
is the orthogonal projection
q_{u}=\mathds{1}-|u\rangle\langle u|
acting on the
i
th particle coordinate
x_{i}
. Here, the partial inner
product is taken w.r.t. the particle coordinates
x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{N}
. The
adjoint of
X_{u,\alpha}
is given by (
1.18
), and it
holds

(2.12)

\displaystyle X_{u,\alpha}^{*}X_{u,\alpha}=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{N}},\qquad X_{u,\alpha}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}^{\leq N}\otimes\mathcal{F}},

in particular
X_{u,\alpha}:\mathcal{H}_{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}^{\leq N}\otimes\mathcal{F}
is unitary. Written in terms of creation
and annihilation operators, the excitation map acts as

(2.13)

\displaystyle X_{u,\alpha}\Psi_{N}=\bigg(\bigoplus_{k=0}^{N}q_{u}^{\otimes k}\frac{b(u)^{N-k}}{\sqrt{(N-k)!}}\bigg)\otimes W^{*}(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Psi_{N}.

This leads to the following useful relations
[
66
,
67
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

As identities on
\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, we have for all
f,g\in\{u\}^{\perp}

\displaystyle X_{u,\alpha}b^{*}(u)b(u)X_{u,\alpha}^{*}

\displaystyle=[N-\mathcal{N}_{b}]_{+},

\displaystyle X_{u,\alpha}b^{*}(f)b(u)X_{u,\alpha}^{*}

\displaystyle=b^{*}(f)\left[N-\mathcal{N}_{b}\right]^{1/2}_{+},

\displaystyle X_{u,\alpha}b^{*}(u)b(f)X_{u,\alpha}^{*}

\displaystyle=\left[N-\mathcal{N}_{b}\right]^{1/2}_{+}b(f),

\displaystyle X_{u,\alpha}b^{*}(f)b(g)X_{u,\alpha}^{*}

\displaystyle=b^{*}(f)b(g),

where
[a]_{+}=\max\{a,0\}
. Moreover, for all
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

\displaystyle X_{u,\alpha}a(h)X_{u,\alpha}^{*}=a(h)+\sqrt{N}\big\langle h,\alpha\big\rangle.

To measure the distance between reduced densities, we introduced the
functional
\beta
in (
1.13
). In Section

3
, we need a second functional given by

(2.14)

\displaystyle\gamma\left[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)\right]=\|\nabla_{1}(q_{u})_{1}\Psi_{N}\|^{2}+N^{-1}\|\text{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega)^{1/2}W^{*}(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Psi_{N}\|^{2}.

Using Lemma
2.1
, they can be expressed in terms of the
excitation map as

(2.15a)

\displaystyle\beta[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{N}\big\langle X_{u,\alpha}\Psi_{N},\mathcal{N}X_{u,\alpha}\Psi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{N}},

(2.15b)

\displaystyle\gamma[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{N}\big\langle X_{u,\alpha}\Psi_{N},\mathbb{T}X_{u,\alpha}\Psi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}.

2.2.
Notation

We recall the Hilbert space

(2.16)

\mathcal{H}_{N}=\bigotimes_{\rm sym}^{N}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\otimes\mathcal{F}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})\otimes\mathcal{F},

and the definitions
\omega(k)=\sqrt{k^{2}+1}
and

(2.17)

\displaystyle G_{x}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega(k)}}e^{-ikx}

and introduce the function

(2.18)

\displaystyle B_{x}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{G_{x}(k)}{k^{2}+\omega(k)}=\frac{e^{-ikx}}{\sqrt{\omega(k)}\left(k^{2}+\omega(k)\right)}.

Moreover, we adopt the following notation conventions.

•

For a normed space
X
we denote its topological dual by
X^{\prime}
.

•

For normed spaces
X,Y
we denote the norm of a linear map
A:X\to Y

by
\|A\|_{X\to Y}
.

•

H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
s\in\mathbb{R}
denotes the non-homogeneous

L^{2}
-Sobolev space.

•

For the norms on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
we write

\|u\|_{L^{2}}
and
\|u\|_{H^{s}}
.

•

\mathfrak{h}_{s}
with
s\in\mathbb{R}
is the weighted
L^{2}
-space
with norm
\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s}}=\|\omega^{s}\alpha\|_{L^{2}}
.

•

\|\cdot\|
denotes the norms of
\mathcal{H}_{N}
and
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, depending on the context.

•

\dot{u}_{t}
denotes the time-derivative of a function
t\mapsto u_{t}
.

•

We do not specify the domain of integration if it is equal to

\mathbb{R}^{3n}
.

•

For the Fourier transform of
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we use the
convention that

(2.19)

\displaystyle\widehat{u}(k)=(2\pi)^{-3/2}\int dx\,e^{-ikx}u(x).

•

For a quadratic form with domain
Q(A)
associated with
A:Q(A)\to Q(A)^{\prime}
, we write
A+\text{h.c.}
for the quadratic form

(2.20)

\langle\psi,(A+\text{h.c.})\psi\rangle=2\text{Re}\langle\psi,A\psi\rangle,\qquad\psi\in Q(A).

•

The letter
C
denotes a generic constant, whose value may change
within a sequence of inequalities. For example, in
X\leq CY\leq CZ

the two occurrences of
C
may represent different numbers.

2.3.
The SKG equations

The next statement recaps the well-posedness theory of the non-linear SKG
equations,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} i\partial_{t}u_{t}(x)&\,=\,\big(-\Delta+\phi_{\alpha_{t}}(x)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big\langle u_{t},\phi_{\alpha_{t}}u_{t}\big\rangle\big)u_{t}(x)\\[4.2679pt]
i\partial_{t}\alpha_{t}(k)&\,=\,\omega(k)\alpha_{t}(k)+\big\langle u_{t},G_{(\cdot)}(k)u_{t}\big\rangle\\[4.2679pt]
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})|_{t=0}&\,=(u,\alpha).\end{aligned}\end{cases}

Proposition 2.2
.

For any
s\geq 0
the Cauchy problem (
2.21
) is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{s-1/2}
. The solutions satisfy
\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}}=\|u\|_{L^{2}}
and, for
s\geq 1
,
\mathcal{E}(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathcal{E}(u,\alpha)
with
\mathcal{E}
defined by (
1.12
).

In addition, for any integer
s\geq 1
, there exists
\delta
such that
for any
M>0
there exists
C
so that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and

\lVert(u,\alpha)\rVert_{H^{s}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{s-1/2}}\leq M
, the
solution
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
satisfies

\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{H^{s}}+\|\alpha_{t}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s-1/2}}\leq\begin{cases}\,C\quad&\text{if}\ s=1\;,\\
\,C(1+\lvert t\rvert)^{\delta}\quad&\text{otherwise}\;.\end{cases}

Proof.

The well-posedness together with the conservation properties is a special
case of
[
82
, Thm.1.4]
; see also
[
3
,
21
]
. The
estimate on the norms for
s=1
follows from the conservation properties
and an application of the bound (
6.4e
).
The polynomial-in-time bounds for
s>1
can be proved by an iterative
argument, adapting the approach of
[
35
]
for the Landau–Pekar
equations, see also
[
51
]
for a different approach.
∎

3.
The mean-field approximation

In this section we study the approximation of
e^{-itH_{N}}
on the level of
reduced densities and prove Theorem
1.1
. We first consider the evolution generated by the Nelson
Hamiltonian after dressing it with a suitable unitary transformation. Even
though the dressed Hamiltonian
H_{N}^{\rm D}
has a more complicated structure
than the original Hamiltonian, it contains less singular interaction terms
and is thus better suited for our analysis. In fact,
H_{N}^{\rm D}
is a
perturbation of the non-interacting Hamiltonian in the sense of quadratic
forms.

In order to approximate the evolution
e^{-itH_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}}
, it is
necessary to replace the mean-field equations by their dressed variant, as
already observed in
[
2
]
. The approximation result for the reduced
densities of
e^{-itH_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}}\Psi_{N}
using the dressed mean-field
equations, with a statement analogous to that of Theorem
1.1
, is given in Theorem
3.2
. We then study the dressing
transformations, and state in Lemma
3.5
that they can be approximated by a mean-field dressing flow in a
similar way. The proof of Theorem
1.1
is given by combining these results in Section
3.4
.

3.1.
Dressed dynamics on the microscopic level

With
B_{x}(k)
given by (
2.18
) and the field operator
\hat{\Phi}

from (
2.6
), we consider the family of unitary dressing
transformations

(3.1)

\displaystyle W^{\rm D}(\theta)=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\exp\bigg(\frac{-i\theta}{\sqrt{N}}\hat{\Phi}(iB_{x_{j}})\bigg)

and set
W^{\mathrm{D}}:=W^{\mathrm{D}}(1)
. This transformation goes back to
Gross and Nelson
[
80
]
, and the following lemma recalls a well-known
relation between the renormalized Nelson Hamiltonian and the dressed Nelson
Hamiltonian.

Lemma 3.1
.

Consider the symmetric quadratic
form defined on the form domain of
\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}(-\Delta_{i})
,

H_{N}^{\rm D}=\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}(-\Delta_{i})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{A}_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}V(x_{i}-x_{j})\\
+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big(a(kB_{x_{i}})^{2}+2a^{*}(kB_{x_{i}})a(kB_{x_{i}})+a^{*}(kB_{x_{i}})^{2}\Big),

where

(3.2a)

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{x}

\displaystyle=-2\big(i\nabla_{x}\cdot a(kB_{x})+a^{*}(kB_{x})\cdot i\nabla_{x}\big),

(3.2b)

\displaystyle V(x)

\displaystyle=-4\mathrm{Re}\langle G_{x},B_{0}\rangle+2\mathrm{Re}\langle\omega B_{x},B_{0}\rangle.

There exists a unique self-adjoint operator
H_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}
,

D(H_{N}^{\mathrm{D}})
whose quadratic form coincides with the above, and we
have

H_{N}=(W^{\mathrm{D}})^{*}H_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}W^{\mathrm{D}}.

Proof.

Formally, this follows from the definition of the Weyl operators and a
direct computation. The precise statement is a corollary to the original
construction of the renormalized Nelson Hamiltonian
[
80
]
, refined
in
[
46
]
. There, one considers the operator
H_{N,K}^{\mathrm{D}}

related to a dressing transformation with an infrared cutoff
K
(as in
Proposition
1.2
). This is
used to bound the interaction terms relative to the form of the
non-interacting operator with bound less than one, for
K
sufficiently
large (see
[
46
, Thm. 3.3]
). Transforming
H_{N,K}^{\mathrm{D}}
with
the dressing transformation on momenta below
K
gives the formula
above. This does not change the form domain by
[
46
, Thm. 4.1,
Lem. C.4]
.
∎

3.2.
Mean-field approximation of the dressed
dynamics

Given the dressed Nelson Hamiltonian
H_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}
, one can derive an
associated mean-field energy by projecting onto states of the product-like
form (
1.8
) with given one-particle functions

u,\alpha
. The dressed mean-field equations, the Hamiltonian equations
associated with this energy, take the form

(3.3)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} i\partial_{t}u_{t}(x)&=h_{u_{t},\alpha_{t}}u_{t}(x)\\[4.2679pt]
i\partial_{t}\alpha_{t}(k)&=\omega(k)\alpha_{t}+2\langle u_{t},kB_{(\cdot)}(k)(-i\nabla+F_{\alpha_{t}})u_{t}\rangle\\[4.2679pt]
(u_{0},\alpha_{0})&=(u,\alpha)\end{aligned}\end{cases}

where

(3.4a)

\displaystyle h_{u,\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\Delta+A_{\alpha}+(F_{\alpha})^{2}+V\ast|u|^{2}-\mu_{u,\alpha},

(3.4b)

\displaystyle A_{\alpha,x}

\displaystyle=2(-i\nabla_{x})\big\langle kB_{x},\alpha\big\rangle+2\overline{\big\langle kB_{x},\alpha\big\rangle}(-i\nabla_{x}),

(3.4c)

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{Re}\big\langle kB_{x},\alpha\big\rangle,

(3.4d)

\displaystyle\mu_{u,\alpha}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\big\langle u,V\ast|u|^{2}u\big\rangle+\mathrm{Re}\big\langle\alpha,f_{u}\big\rangle+\mathrm{Re}\big\langle\alpha,g_{u,\alpha}\big\rangle,

(3.4e)

\displaystyle f_{u}(k)

\displaystyle=2\big\langle u,kB_{(\cdot)}(k)(-i\nabla)u\big\rangle,

(3.4f)

\displaystyle g_{u,\alpha}(k)

\displaystyle=2\big\langle u,kB_{(\cdot)}(k)F_{\alpha}u\big\rangle.

We denote the associated flow by
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)=(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
, that is
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
solves (
3.3
) with
initial conditions
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})|_{t=0}=(u,\alpha)
(existence of this
flow is the special case
\theta=1
of
Lemma
5.1
).

In the next statement we compare the evolution generated by
H_{N}^{\rm D}

with the dressed mean-field flow
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]
. To this end, we
recall (
1.13
) for the definition of the functional

\beta
and

(3.5)

\displaystyle\gamma\left[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)\right]=\|\nabla_{1}(q_{u})_{1}\Psi_{N}\|^{2}+N^{-1}\|\text{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega)^{1/2}W^{*}(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Psi_{N}\|^{2}.

Essentially,
\gamma
is the mean kinetic energy of particles outside the
condensate state
u
and field modes outside of the coherent state

W(\sqrt{N}\alpha)\Omega
. Also note that by Lemma

3.1
we have
e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N}=(W^{\rm D})^{*}e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N}
, which explains why we now consider
initial states of the form
W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N}
.

The following statement is the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1

and let
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)=(u_{t},\alpha_{t})
denote
the solution to (
5.2
) for initial
conditions
(u,\alpha)
. There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all

\Psi_{N}\in D(H_{N}^{1/2})
with
\|\Psi_{N}\|=1
,
N\geq 1
, and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
we have

\beta\big[e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)\big]\\[2.84526pt]
\leq e^{CR^{\rm D}(t)}\big(\beta\big[W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)\big]+\gamma\big[W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)\big]+N^{-1}\big)

where
R^{\rm D}(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}\|u_{s}\|^{2}_{H^{3}}(1+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}ds
.

To prepare the proof of the theorem, we introduce the fluctuation generator
associated with
e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]
. Recalling the definition of the excitation map (
2.11
), and fixing
\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}
,
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
, we consider the fluctuation vector

(3.6)

\displaystyle\chi^{\rm D}(t):=X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)}e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N}.

A simple computation shows that
\chi^{\rm D}(t)
satisfies the equation

i\partial_{t}\chi^{\rm D}(t)=H^{{\rm D},\leq N}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi^{\rm D}(t)
with

(3.7)

\displaystyle H^{{\rm D},\leq N}_{u,\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle=i\dot{X}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)}(X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)})^{*}+X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)}H_{N}^{\rm D}(X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)})^{*}.

Note that
H^{{\rm D},\leq N}_{u,\alpha}(t)
maps
\mathcal{F}^{\leq N}_{\perp u_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
into
\mathcal{F}^{\leq N}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, but for convenience, we will
write it as the restriction of a symmetric operator
H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t):\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, that is

(3.8)

\displaystyle H^{{\rm D},\leq N}_{u,\alpha}(t)=H_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)\restriction\mathcal{F}^{\leq N}_{\perp u_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{F}.

The explicit expression for
H_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)
is given in Section

6.4
. The fluctuation vector
\chi^{\rm D}(t)

then satisfies the Schrödinger type equation

(3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} i\partial_{t}\chi^{\rm D}(t)&=H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi^{\rm D}(t)\\[2.84526pt]
\chi^{\rm D}(0)&=X_{u,\alpha}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N}.\end{aligned}\end{cases}

In Lemma
6.11
we shall prove the following bounds

(3.10a)

\displaystyle\pm\big(H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)-\mathbb{T}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{T}+C(\mathcal{N}+1)(1+\tfrac{1}{N}\mathcal{N}_{b})^{2},

(3.10b)

\displaystyle\pm i[\mathcal{N},H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)]

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{T}+C(\mathcal{N}+1)(1+\tfrac{1}{N}\mathcal{N}_{b})^{2},

(3.10c)

\displaystyle\pm\tfrac{d}{dt}H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{T}+C\rho(t)(\mathcal{N}+1)(1+\tfrac{1}{N}\mathcal{N}_{b})^{2},

where
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathbb{T}
are defined as in (
2.5
),
\rho(t)=\|u_{t}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|\alpha_{t}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}
and
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)
. Equipped with these estimates we can now come to the proof of Theorem

3.2
, whose strategy
is inspired by
[
15
,
76
,
59
]
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Consider
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)
and the fluctuation vector
\chi^{\rm D}(t)
given by
(
3.6
) for initial states
(u,\alpha)
and
\Psi_{N}

as stated in the hypothesis. Note that by definition,
\chi^{\rm D}(t)\in\mathcal{F}^{\leq N}_{\perp u_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
. Relations
(
2.15a
) and (
2.15b
) imply that

(3.11a)

\displaystyle\beta[e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{N}\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),\mathcal{N}\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle

(3.11b)

\displaystyle\gamma[e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{N}\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),\mathbb{T}\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle.

From (
3.10a
) and
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}\chi^{\rm D}(t)=\chi^{\rm D}(t)
it follows that

\displaystyle N\beta[e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)]\leq\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),(\mathbb{T}+\mathcal{N})\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle

(3.12)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq 2\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle+C\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),(\mathcal{N}+1)\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle=:f(t).

We proceed by estimating the time-derivative of
f(t)
in order to conclude
via Grönwall’s inequality. Using (
3.9
) and with
the aid of
(
3.10a
)–(
3.10c
), one
computes

\displaystyle\big|\dot{f}(t)\big|

\displaystyle=\big|2\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),(\tfrac{d}{dt}H_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t))\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle+C\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),i[H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t),\mathcal{N}]\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),\mathbb{T}\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle+C\rho(t)\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),(\mathcal{N}+1)\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),H_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle+C\rho(t)\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),(\mathcal{N}+1)\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle

(3.13)

\displaystyle\leq C\rho(t)f(t),

where we used that
\rho(t)\geq 1
. Grönwall’s inequality thus implies
f(t)\leq e^{C\int_{0}^{|t|}\rho(s)ds}f(0)
and using again
(
3.10a
) together with
(
3.11a
), (
3.11b
), we arrive
at

\displaystyle\beta[e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)]\leq Ce^{C\int_{0}^{|t|}\rho(s)ds}\tfrac{1}{N}\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(0),(\mathbb{T}+\mathcal{N}+1)\chi^{\rm D}(0)\big\rangle

(3.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt=e^{CR^{\mathrm{D}}(t)}\big(\beta[W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)]+\gamma[W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)]+N^{-1}\big).

This completes the proof of Theorem
3.2
.
∎

3.3.
Mean-field approximation of the dressing transformation

The dressing transformation
W^{\mathrm{D}}
is generated by an operator that
looks like the interaction term of the Nelson Hamiltonian, but has the
regular form factor
iB_{x}
. There are thus mean-field equations associated to
the dynamics
\theta\mapsto W^{\mathrm{D}}(\theta)
, (
3.1
), as in the case of the (dressed) Nelson
Hamiltonian. The mean-field equations corresponding to the dressing
transformation are given by

(3.15)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} i\partial_{\theta}u^{\theta}(x)&=\tau_{u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta}}(x)u^{\theta}(x)\\[2.84526pt]
\partial_{\theta}\alpha^{\theta}(k)&=B_{0}(k)\widehat{|u^{\theta}|^{2}}(k)\\[2.84526pt]
(u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta})|_{\theta=0}&=(u,\alpha)\end{aligned}\end{cases}

where we introduced

(3.16)

\displaystyle\tau_{u,\alpha}(x)=\widetilde{\phi}_{\alpha}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\big\langle u,\widetilde{\phi}_{\alpha}u\big\rangle,\quad\widetilde{\phi}_{\alpha}(x)=2\mathrm{Re}\big\langle iB_{x},\alpha\big\rangle.

We denote by
\mathfrak{D}[\theta]
the flow corresponding to this equation,
i.e.

(3.17)

\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)=(u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta}),

where
(u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta})
is the solution to (
3.15
) with initial condition
(u,\alpha)
. Being the flow of
an autonomous system of equations, we have
\mathfrak{D}[\theta]\circ\mathfrak{D}[-\theta]=1
. For
\theta=1
we use the shorthand

\mathfrak{D}:=\mathfrak{D}[1]
.

In fact,
\mathfrak{D}[\theta]
can be determined explicitly
following
[
2
, Lem. III.11]
. Since
\tau_{u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta}}

is real, the solution satisfies
|u^{\theta}|^{2}=|u|^{2}
, and then the
equation for
\alpha
can be solved for

(3.18)

\alpha^{\theta}(k)=\alpha(k)+\theta B_{0}(k)\widehat{|u|^{2}}(k).

Since
B_{0}
is an even function,

(3.19)

\mathrm{Re}\big\langle iB_{x},B_{0}\widehat{|u|^{2}}\big\rangle=\mathrm{Im}\int dy\widehat{B_{0}^{2}}(y-x)|u|^{2}(y)=0.

Hence, we have
\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha^{\theta}}=\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha}
, and one can
simplify the equations using
\tau_{u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta}}=\tau_{u,\alpha}
. The system of ordinary differential
equations (
3.15
) for each
(x,k)
is then
solved explicitly by

(3.20)

(u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta}):=\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)=\Big(e^{-i\theta\tau_{u,\alpha}}u,\alpha+\theta B_{0}(\cdot)\widehat{|u|^{2}}\Big).

The flow
\mathfrak{D}[\theta]
preserves the relevant spaces of Cauchy data
for the SKG equations (cf. Proposition
2.2
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
n-1<s<n+1
. There exists
C
so that for
all
(u,\alpha)\in H^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{s}
and
|\theta|\leq 1

the functions
(u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta})=\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)

satisfy

\displaystyle\|u^{\theta}\|_{H^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|_{H^{n}}\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s}}^{n}

\displaystyle\|\alpha^{\theta}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|u\|_{H^{n}}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s}}).

Proof.

We use the explicit form (
3.17
) of
\mathfrak{D}[\theta]
and some straightforward estimates (some of which will be proved
in Section
6.2
). We have for
m\leq n

(3.21)

e^{i\theta\tau_{u,\alpha}}\nabla^{m}u^{\theta}=(\nabla+i\theta\nabla\tau_{u,\alpha})^{m}u.

With
\nabla\tau_{u,\alpha}(x)=-2\mathrm{Re}\langle kB_{x},\alpha\rangle
we
thus have

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{m}u^{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\sum_{j_{1}+\dots+j_{\ell}\leq m}\Big\|\Big(\prod_{i=0}^{\ell}\langle|k|^{j_{i}}B_{0},|\alpha|\rangle\Big)\nabla^{m-\sum j_{i}}u\Big\|_{L^{2}}

(3.22)

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|_{H^{m}}\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s}}^{m},

by Lemma
6.3
(where we used that
s>n-1
). For

\alpha^{\theta}
we have

(3.23)

\|\alpha^{\theta}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s}}\leq\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s}}+|\theta|\|B_{0}(k)\widehat{|u|^{2}}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s}}\leq\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{s}}+|\theta|\||u|^{2}\|_{H^{s-5/2}}.

For
n=1
we simply bound the last term by
\|u\|_{L^{4}}^{2}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
, and for
n\geq 2
we use that

(3.24)

\|u^{2}\|_{H^{s-5/2}}\leq\|u^{2}\|_{H^{n}}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{n}}^{2}.

This proves the claim.
∎

To connect the statements of Theorems
3.2
and
1.1
,
we make use of the fact that the dressing flow
\mathfrak{D}
interpolates
between the SKG flow (
2.21
)
and the dressed mean-field flow (
3.3
). This is a direct
consequence of Lemma
5.1
for
\theta=1
.

Lemma 3.4
.

For all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we
have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{D}\circ\mathfrak{s}[t].

The next lemma is an analogue to Theorem
3.2
for the dynamics
W^{\mathrm{D}}(\theta)
in
“time”
\theta
.

Lemma 3.5
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
(u,\alpha)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{0}
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
,
\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}
with
\|\Psi_{N}\|=1
,
N\geq 1
, and
|\theta|\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\beta\big[W^{\rm D}(\theta)\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)\big]

\displaystyle\leq e^{C(\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}})}\big(\beta\big[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)]+N^{-1}\big).

To prove the lemma, we introduce, in close analogy to the discussion after
Theorem
3.2
, the
fluctuation generator associated with
W^{\rm D}(\theta)
and
\mathfrak{D}[\theta]
. For
\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}
and
(u,\alpha)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{0}
, we consider the fluctuation vector

(3.25)

\displaystyle\zeta(\theta):={X}_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)}W^{\rm D}(\theta)\Psi_{N}.

A short computation shows that
i\partial_{\theta}\zeta(\theta)=D^{\leq N}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\zeta_{N}(\theta)
with

(3.26)

D_{u,\alpha}^{\leq N}(\theta)=X_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)}\bigg(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\hat{\Phi}(iB_{x_{j}})\bigg)(X_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)})^{*}\\
+i\left(\partial_{\theta}X_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)}\right)(X_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)})^{*}.

As before, it is convenient to write
D^{\leq N}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)=D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\restriction\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}^{\leq N}\otimes\mathcal{F}
as the
restriction of a symmetric operator
D_{u,\alpha}(\theta):\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
. After calculating
D_{u,\alpha}^{\leq N}(\theta)
by a
straightforward application of Lemma
2.1
, we make the
choice

\displaystyle D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{b}\big(\tau_{u,\alpha}\big)+\bigg(\int dxdk\,\kappa_{u^{\theta}}(k,x)a_{k}^{*}b_{x}^{*}\big[1-\tfrac{\mathcal{N}_{b}}{N}]_{+}^{1/2}+\textnormal{ h.c.}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad-\bigg(\int dxdk\,\kappa_{u^{\theta}}(-k,x)a_{k}b_{x}^{*}\big[1-\tfrac{\mathcal{N}_{b}}{N}]_{+}^{1/2}+\textnormal{ h.c.}\bigg)

(3.27)

\displaystyle\quad+N^{-1/2}\int dx\,b_{x}^{*}\Big(q_{u^{\theta}}\hat{\Phi}(iB_{x})q_{u^{\theta}}-\big\langle u^{\theta},\hat{\Phi}(iB_{(\cdot)})u^{\theta}\big\rangle\Big)b_{x},

where
\tau_{u,\alpha}
is defined by (
3.16
) and

(3.28)

\kappa_{u}(k,x)=\big(q_{u}iB_{(\cdot)}(k)u\big)(x).

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

Consider the fluctuation vector
\zeta(\theta)
given by
(
3.25
). Using
(
2.15a
), we can express the relevant
\beta

functional as

(3.29)

\displaystyle\beta[W^{\rm D}(\theta)\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{N}\big\langle\zeta(\theta),\mathcal{N}\zeta(\theta)\big\rangle.

We use
i\partial_{\theta}\zeta(\theta)=D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\zeta(\theta)
, with
D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)
given by
(
3.3
), in combination with the commutator
bound (which is stated precisely and proved in Lemma

6.13
)

(3.30)

\displaystyle\pm i[\mathcal{N},D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)]

\displaystyle\leq C(\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}})(\mathcal{N}+1)(1+(\tfrac{1}{N}\mathcal{N}_{b})^{1/2}).

Together with
\zeta(\theta)=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}\zeta(\theta)
,
this implies

(3.31)

\displaystyle\big|\tfrac{d}{d\theta}\big\langle\zeta(\theta),\mathcal{N}\zeta(\theta)\big\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq C(\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}})\big\langle\zeta(\theta),(\mathcal{N}+1)\zeta(\theta)\big\rangle

and by applying Grönwall’s inequality, we obtain for
|\theta|\leq 1

(3.32)

\displaystyle\big\langle\zeta(\theta),(\mathcal{N}+1)\zeta(\theta)\big\rangle\leq e^{C|\theta|(\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}})}\big\langle\zeta(0),(\mathcal{N}+1)\zeta(0)\big\rangle.

In combination with (
3.29
) and the fact
that
\|\zeta(0)\|=1
, we can derive the desired bound.
∎

As a final preparation for the proof of Theorem
1.1
, the following lemma gives an upper bound for the
functional
\gamma
, defined in (
2.14
), when
evaluated for the dressed states
W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N}
and
\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)
in terms of the energy difference of the microscopic and
mean-field models evaluated in the states
\Psi_{N}
and
(u,\alpha)
, without
dressing.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
\mathcal{E}
be
given by (
1.12
). There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\Psi_{N}\in D(H_{N}^{1/2})
with
\|\Psi_{N}\|=1
,
N\geq 1
, we have

\ \gamma\big[W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)\big]\leq C\Big(\left|N^{-1}\big\langle\Psi_{N},H_{N}\Psi_{N}\big\rangle-\mathcal{E}(u,\alpha)\right|\\
+\max_{j=1,2}(\beta\left[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)\right]+N^{-1})^{j/2}\Big).

For the proof of Lemma
3.6
, which is given in
Section
6.5
, it is important to note that the
strategy used to prove Theorem
3.2
does not work. The reason for this is that the operator

\mathbb{T}
is not dominated by the generator
D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)
, and
hence we do not have analogous estimates to
(
3.10a
)–(
3.10c
) at our
disposal. Therefore we rely on a different type of energy estimates,
motivated by ideas from
[
56
]
.

3.4.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Combining the results of the previous two sections, we can prove our first
main theorem.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

For
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
, denote by

\displaystyle(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha),\quad(u^{\mathrm{D}},\alpha^{\mathrm{D}})=\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha),\quad(u^{\mathrm{D}}_{t},\alpha^{\mathrm{D}}_{t})=\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)

the solutions of the SKG equations (
2.21
), and the dressed SKG equations (
3.3
)
with initial data transformed by the mean-field dressing
transformation (
3.17
). Also recall that by Lemma

3.4

(3.33)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u^{\mathrm{D}},\alpha^{\mathrm{D}}).

Since
W^{\rm D}(-1)W^{\rm D}(1)=\mathds{1}
and
\mathfrak{D}[-1]\circ\mathfrak{D}=\mathds{1}
, we can use Lemma
3.5
for
\theta=-1
and with
\Psi_{N}\to W^{\rm D}e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N}
, and
(u,\alpha)\to\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)
, to get

(3.34)

\beta[e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N},(u_{t},\alpha_{t})]\\
\leq e^{C(\|u^{\rm D}_{t}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha_{t}^{\rm D}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}})}\left(\beta\big[W^{\rm D}e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{D}(u_{t},\alpha_{t})\big]+N^{-1}\right).

The exponential factor is uniformly bounded in
t
, since by Lemma

3.3
and Proposition
2.2

(3.35a)

\displaystyle\|u^{\rm D}_{t}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|u_{t}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\|\alpha_{t}\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}}\leq C\,,

(3.35b)

\displaystyle\|\alpha^{\rm D}_{t}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|u_{t}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha_{t}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}})\leq C.

In view of
H_{N}=(W^{\rm D})^{*}H_{N}^{\rm D}W^{\rm D}
(see Lemma

3.1
) and (
3.33
), we can proceed using
Theorem
3.2
to
estimate

(3.36)

\beta\big[e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u^{\mathrm{D}},\alpha^{\mathrm{D}})\big]\\[2.84526pt]
\leq e^{CR^{\rm D}(t)}\big(\beta\big[W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},(u^{\mathrm{D}},\alpha^{\mathrm{D}})\big]+\gamma\big[W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},(u^{\mathrm{D}},\alpha^{\mathrm{D}})\big]+N^{-1}\big),

where
R^{\mathrm{D}}(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}\|u^{\mathrm{D}}_{s}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|\alpha^{\mathrm{D}}_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}ds
. The
\beta
functional on
the right side is estimated with the aid of Lemma
3.5
,

(3.37)

\displaystyle\beta\big[W^{\rm D}\Psi_{N},\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)\big]

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\beta\big[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)]+N^{-1}\big).

The
\gamma
functional is bounded by Lemma
3.6
,
which yields altogether

(3.38)

\beta[e^{-itH_{N}}\Psi_{N},(u_{t},\alpha_{t})]\leq e^{CR^{\rm D}(t)}\Big(\left|N^{-1}\big\langle\Psi_{N},H_{N}\Psi_{N}\big\rangle-\mathcal{E}(u,\alpha)\right|\\
+\max_{j=1,2}\big(\beta\big[\Psi_{N},(u,\alpha)\big]+N^{-1}\big]\big)^{j/2}\Big).

It remains to relate the time-dependent pre-factor to the solution of the
SKG equation. Using Lemma
3.3
and the fact
that
(u_{s}^{\mathrm{D}},\alpha^{\mathrm{D}}_{s})=\mathfrak{D}(u_{s},\alpha_{s})

we have

(3.39)

\displaystyle\|u^{\mathrm{D}}_{s}\|_{H^{3}}(1+\|\alpha^{\mathrm{D}}_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})

\displaystyle\leq C\|u_{s}\|_{H^{3}}\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}}^{3}(1+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}}+\|u_{s}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}).

By Proposition
2.2
we have
\|u_{s}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq C
. Young’s inequality then yields

(3.40)

R^{\rm D}(t)\leq 1+C\int_{0}^{|t|}(\|u_{s}\|_{H^{3}}^{10}+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}}^{10})ds,

and this proves the claim.
∎

4.
Bogoliubov theory and the norm approximation

In this section we study the Bogoliubov approximation of
e^{-itH_{N}}
and
prove Theorem
1.5
. Our strategy is similar to the
case of the mean-field approximation discussed in
Section
3
. We start by introducing the dressed Bogoliubov
Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}^{\mathrm{D}}_{u,\alpha}(t)
and the associated Fock
space evolution
\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)
, which describe the
fluctuations around the mean-field solution for the dressed dynamics. In
Theorem
4.2
we provide a statement
analogous to Theorem
1.5
for the dressed case. In
Section
4.2
we then study the norm approximation
of the dressing transformation, which is given again in terms of a suitable
Bogoliubov type evolution. This evolution describes the fluctuations with
