respect to the mean-field dressing
\mathfrak{D}[\theta]
. We use the norm
approximation of the dressing to relate the statements of Theorem

1.5
and

4.2
. For that purpose, it is
crucial to observe that the Bogoliubov approximation of
W^{\rm D}(\theta)

in fact interpolates between the dressed and undressed Bogoliubov
evolutions. This is stated in Proposition
4.6
, whose
proof is given in Section
5
.

4.1.
Norm approximation of the dressed
dynamics

We introduce the Bogoliubov evolution describing the fluctuations associated
with the dressed Nelson Hamiltonian
H_{N}^{\rm D}
and the dressed mean-field
equations (
5.2
). To this end, we
consider the quadratic approximation of the fluctuation generator

H_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)
introduced in (
3.7
)
(see Lemma
6.10
for the explicit form of
H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)
). For
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
and
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)
, we
introduce the quadratic operator acting on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
given by

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{b}(h_{t})+\mathbb{K}^{(1)}_{u_{t}}+\big(\mathbb{K}^{(2)}_{u_{t}}+\text{h.c.}\big)+\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega)

\displaystyle\ +\int dxdk\Big(\big(q_{u_{t}}L_{\alpha_{t}}(k)u_{t}\big)(x)a^{*}_{k}b_{x}^{*}+\big(q_{u_{t}}L_{\alpha_{t}}(k)^{*}u_{t}\big)(x)a_{k}b_{x}^{*}\Big)+\text{h.c.}

\displaystyle\ +\int dkdl\Big(-2M_{u_{t}}(k,-l)a^{*}_{k}a_{l}+M_{u_{t}}(k,l)a_{k}^{*}a_{l}^{*}+M_{u_{t}}(-k,-l)a_{k}a_{l}\Big)

with
h_{t}=h_{u_{t},\alpha_{t}}
as defined in
(
3.4a
),

(4.2a)

\displaystyle\big(L_{\alpha}(k)u\big)(x)

\displaystyle=2kB_{x}(k)\big((-i\nabla+F_{\alpha}(x))u\big)(x),

(4.2b)

\displaystyle M_{u}(k,l)

\displaystyle=\big\langle u,kB_{(\cdot)}(k)\cdot lB_{(\cdot)}(l)u\big\rangle,

with
F_{\alpha}
given by (
3.4c
), and

(4.2c)

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}^{(1)}_{u}

\displaystyle=\int dxdy\,K_{u}^{(1)}(x,y)b^{*}_{x}b_{y},\quad\mathbb{K}^{(2)}_{u}=\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\,K_{u}^{(2)}(x,y)b^{*}_{x}b^{*}_{y}

where

(4.2d)

\displaystyle K_{u}^{(1)}=q_{u}\widetilde{K}^{(1)}_{u}q_{u},\qquad\widetilde{K}_{u}^{(1)}(x,y)

\displaystyle=u(x)V(x-y)\overline{u(y)},

(4.2e)

\displaystyle K_{u}^{(2)}=(q_{u}\otimes q_{u})\widetilde{K}^{(2)}_{u},\qquad\widetilde{K}_{u}^{(2)}(x,y)

\displaystyle=u(x)V(x-y)u(y)

with
q_{u}=1-|u\rangle\langle u|
and
V(x)
defined in
(
3.2b
).

The next proposition on the evolution generated by the operator
\mathbb{H}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)
is the special case
\theta=1
,
\Lambda=\infty
of
Proposition
5.2
below.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
and let
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)
be given by (
3.3
). For every
\Psi\in D((\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2})
there exists a unique solution to the
Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} i\partial_{t}\Psi(t)&=\mathbb{H}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)\Psi(t)\\
\Psi(0)&=\Psi_{0}\end{aligned}\end{cases}

such that
\Psi\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F})\cap L_{\rm{\mathrm{loc}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},D((\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2}))
. The solution map
\Psi_{0}\mapsto\Psi(t)
extends to a unitary
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)
on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
satisfying
\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)(\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F})\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\perp u_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
. Moreover, for every

\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there is a constant
C(\ell)
such that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)^{*}(\mathbb{T}+\mathcal{N}+1)\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle\leq e^{C(1)R^{\rm D}(t)}(\mathbb{T}+\mathcal{N}+1),

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)^{*}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}\,\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)

\displaystyle\leq e^{C(\ell)R^{\rm D}(t)}\,(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}.

in the sense of quadratic forms on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, with
R^{\rm D}(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}\|u_{s}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}ds
.

The next theorem is our main statement of this section, making precise that
the evolution
\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)
describes the fluctuations of
the dressed Nelson dynamics around the dressed mean-field solutions.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
, and
\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)
the unitary
defined in Proposition
4.1
. There exists a constant
C>0

such that for all
\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
with

\|\chi\|=1
,
N\geq 1
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\Big\|e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi-X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi\Big\|\leq e^{CR^{\rm D}(t)}\delta^{\rm D}(\chi)^{1/2}\frac{\sqrt{\log N}}{N^{1/4}},

where
R^{\rm D}(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}\|u_{s}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}ds
with
(u_{s},\alpha_{s})=\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[s](u,\alpha)
and

\delta^{\rm D}(\chi)=\|(1+\mathcal{N}^{3}+\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{b}(-\Delta)+\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega))^{1/2}\chi\|^{2}.

The proof of the theorem relies on a technical bound on the difference of

H_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)
and its quadratic approximation
\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)
in terms of the operators
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathbb{T}
. In details, there exists a constant
C>0
, such that for all
\chi\in\mathcal{F}^{\leq N}\otimes\mathcal{F}
,
\phi\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\big|\big\langle\chi,\left(H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)-\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)\right)\phi\big\rangle\big|

(4.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq C\rho(t)\frac{\ln N}{N^{1/2}}\|\left(\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T}+1\right)^{1/2}\chi\|\|\left(\mathcal{N}^{3}+\mathbb{T}+1\right)^{1/2}\phi\|

with
\rho(t)=\|u_{t}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|\alpha_{t}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}
. The
precise statement and its proof are given in Lemma

6.12
. Note that we choose to distribute
the higher moments of
\mathcal{N}
unequally in
(
4.1
) because below we will rely on the estimates
provided by Theorem
3.2
and Proposition
4.1
that control the higher moments
of
\mathcal{N}
during the Bogoliubov dynamics but only its first moment
during the many-body evolution.

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Using that the excitation map is isometric, we can write the norm
difference as

(4.4)

\displaystyle\big\|e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi-X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)\big\|=\big\|\chi^{\rm D}(t)-\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi\big\|,

where
\chi^{\rm D}(t)=X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t](u,\alpha)}e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi
and by (
2.12
),

\chi^{\rm D}(0)=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}\chi
. Since
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}\chi^{\rm D}(t)=\chi^{\rm D}(t)
we can omit the projection on
the right-hand side by increasing the value of the norm. Recalling
(
3.9
), we now use
i\partial_{t}\chi^{\rm D}(t)=H^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi^{\rm D}(t)
and Proposition
4.1

to obtain

(4.5)

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dt}\|\chi^{\rm D}(t)-\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)\chi\|^{2}=2\mathrm{Im}\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),(H_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)-\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t))\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)\chi\big\rangle.

Using again
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}\chi^{\rm D}(t)=\chi^{\rm D}(t)
,
we can apply (
4.1
) to bound the right-hand side,
so that

(4.6)

\tfrac{d}{dt}\|\chi^{\rm D}(t)-\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)\chi\|^{2}\\
\leq C\rho(t)\|\left(\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T}+1\right)^{1/2}\chi^{\rm D}(t)\|\|\left(\mathcal{N}^{3}+\mathbb{T}+1\right)^{1/2}\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)\chi\|\frac{\ln N}{\sqrt{N}}.

By Proposition
4.1
, we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{N}^{3}+\mathbb{T}+1)^{1/2}\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)\chi\|\leq e^{CR^{\mathrm{D}}(t)}\|(\mathcal{N}^{3}+\mathbb{T}+1)^{1/2}\chi\|

and by the same argument as in the proof of Theorem

3.2
,

(4.8)

\displaystyle\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(t),(\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T}+1)\chi^{\rm D}(t)\big\rangle\leq e^{CR^{\mathrm{D}}(t)}\big\langle\chi^{\rm D}(0),(\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T}+1)\chi^{\rm D}(0)\big\rangle.

Since
\|\chi^{\rm D}(0)-\chi\|\leq N^{-1}\|\mathcal{N}^{1/2}\chi\|^{2}
, this
implies the desired bound

(4.9)

\displaystyle\|\chi^{\rm D}(t)-\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\rm D}(t)\chi\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{CR^{\mathrm{D}}(t)}\|(\mathcal{N}^{3}+\mathbb{T}+1)^{1/2}\chi\|^{2}N^{-1/2}\ln N.

which concludes the proof of Theorem

4.2
.
∎

4.2.
Norm approximation of the dressing
transformation

We now consider the dressing transformation on the level of the
fluctuations. The effective dressing transformation is used for two
purposes. First, it allows for a norm approximation of
W^{\rm D}(\theta)

and, second, it provides an interpolation between the undressed and dressed
Bogoliubov evolutions. Since the undressed and the dressed Bogoliubov
Hamiltonians are both quadratic operators on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, see
(
1.22
) and (
4.1
), it is natural to
choose the dressing transformation that interpolates between the two itself
as an evolution generated by a quadratic operator. The right candidate for
this is the Bogoliubov type approximation of the microscopic dressing
W^{\rm D}(\theta)
associated with the mean-field flow
\mathfrak{D}[\theta]
(more
precisely, the evolution generated by the quadratic approximation of the
fluctuation generator (
3.3
)).

For
(u,\alpha)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

(u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta})=\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)
,
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
, consider the quadratic operator

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)=\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{b}(\tau_{u,\alpha})+\bigg(\int dxdk\,\big(\kappa_{u^{\theta}}^{\Lambda}(k,x)a^{*}_{k}b_{x}^{*}-\kappa^{\Lambda}_{u^{\theta}}(-k,x)a_{k}b^{*}_{x}\big)+\text{h.c.}\bigg)

with
\tau_{u,\alpha}
defined in (
3.16
) and

(4.11)

\kappa^{\Lambda}_{u}(k,x)=\big(q_{u}iB^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)u\big)(x),\quad B^{\Lambda}_{x}(k)=\mathds{1}_{|k|\leq\Lambda}B_{x}(k).

Since the unitary generated by
\mathbb{D}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}
will play an
important role in the renormalization of the Nelson–Bogoliubov Hamiltonian
in Section
5
, we introduce the kernel

\kappa^{\Lambda}_{u}
with a cutoff
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
.
The next proposition states the existence and some important properties of
this unitary evolution.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
and let
(u^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta})=\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)
denote the solution to (
3.15
) with initial datum
(u,\alpha)
. For every
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
and
\Psi_{0}\in D(\mathcal{N}^{1/2})
there exists a
unique solution to the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} i\partial_{\theta}\Psi(\theta)&=\mathbb{D}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)\Psi(\theta)\\
\Psi(0)&=\Psi_{0}\end{aligned}\end{cases}

such that
\Psi\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F})\cap L_{\rm{loc}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},D(\mathcal{N}^{1/2}))
. The solution map
\Psi_{0}\mapsto\Psi(\theta)
defines a unitary
\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)

on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
with the following properties

(i)

\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)(\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}_{\perp u^{\theta}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
.

(ii)

(u,\alpha,\Lambda)\mapsto\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)
is strongly continuous.

(iii)

\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)
is a
Bogoliubov transformation.

(iv)

For every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
constant
C(\ell)>0
such that for all
(u,\alpha)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|u\|_{{L^{2}}}=1
,
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
and
|\theta|\leq 1
,

(4.12a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)^{*}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}\,\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\leq e^{C(\ell)(\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}})}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}

(4.12b)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}\,\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)^{*}\leq e^{C(\ell)(\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}})}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}

in the sense of quadratic forms on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Existence and uniqueness of the solution follow from
[
66
, Theorem
8]
in combination with the bounds from
Lemma
6.13
. As a consequence, there exists
a two-parameter flow
\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
that
for
\theta,\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta,\theta^{\prime\prime})\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
. Indeed,
define

\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta^{\prime}+\vartheta,\theta^{\prime})\Psi(\theta^{\prime})
to
be the solution of
i\partial_{\vartheta}\chi(\vartheta)=\mathbb{D}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta^{\prime}+\vartheta)\chi(\vartheta)
with

\chi(0)=\Psi(\theta^{\prime})
. Then the flow property follows from uniqueness of
the solution, since

\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\Psi(\theta^{\prime})
and

\Psi(\theta)
are both solutions that agree at
\theta=\theta^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathbb{D}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)
defines a symmetric quadratic
form on
D(\mathcal{N}^{1/2})
, the flow maps

\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
are unitary.

To show the mapping property, consider the orthogonal projection

\Gamma(q_{u^{\theta}})
defined by
\Gamma(q_{u^{\theta}})\restriction\mathcal{F}^{(n)}\otimes\mathcal{F}=(q_{u^{\theta}})^{\otimes n}\otimes 1
. Since
\Gamma(q_{u^{\theta}})(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}_{\perp u^{\theta}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, proving that for all
\Psi\in D(\mathcal{N}^{1/2})

(4.13)

\displaystyle i\tfrac{d}{d\theta}\|\Gamma(q_{u^{\theta}})\Psi(\theta)\|^{2}=-\big\langle\Psi(\theta),[\mathbb{D}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)-\text{d}\Gamma(\tau_{u,\alpha}),\Gamma(q_{u^{\theta}})]\Psi(\theta)\big\rangle=0

will imply
\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)(\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{F}_{\perp u^{\theta}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
. This holds, since

\kappa^{\Lambda}_{u^{\theta}}(k,\cdot)\in\text{Ran}(q_{u^{\theta}})
and thus

(4.14)

\displaystyle\Big[\int dkdx\,\kappa_{u^{\theta}}^{\Lambda}(k,x)a_{k}^{*}b_{x}^{*},\Gamma(q_{u^{\theta}})\Big]

\displaystyle=\int dk\,a_{k}^{*}\Big[b^{*}(\kappa^{\Lambda}_{u^{\theta}}(k,\cdot)),\Gamma(q_{u^{\theta}})\Big]

\displaystyle=\int dk\,a_{k}^{*}b^{*}\big((1-q_{u^{\theta}})\kappa^{\Lambda}_{u^{\theta}}(k,\cdot)\big)\Gamma(q_{u^{\theta}})=0,

with similar calculations for the other terms in the commutator. Applying
the same argument to
\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)^{*}=\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)|_{\theta^{\prime}=0}
shows that

\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\perp u^{\theta}}\otimes\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, which gives the equality.

We now prove continuity of
(u,\alpha,\Lambda)\mapsto\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)\Psi
for every
\Psi\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
,

\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
. By uniqueness of the solution, Duhamel’s formula

\displaystyle\big\langle\Phi,(1-\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)^{*}\mathbb{W}_{u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}^{\Lambda^{\prime}}(\theta))\Psi\big\rangle

(4.15)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad=-i\int_{0}^{\theta}d\eta\big\langle\Phi,\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\eta)^{*}\big(\mathbb{D}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\eta)-\mathbb{D}^{\Lambda^{\prime}}_{u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}(\eta)\big)\mathbb{W}_{u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}^{\Lambda^{\prime}}(\eta))\Psi\big\rangle

holds for all
\Psi,\Phi\in D(\mathcal{N}^{1/2})
. As
(u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\to(u,\alpha,\Lambda)
,
\tau_{u,\alpha}
tends to
\tau_{u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\kappa_{u^{\prime}}^{\Lambda^{\prime}}
tends to

\kappa_{u}^{\Lambda}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
, as one easily verifies. Thus,

\mathbb{D}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\eta)-\mathbb{D}_{u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}^{\Lambda^{\prime}}(\eta)
tends to zero as quadratic form
on
D(\mathcal{N}^{1/2})
and since
\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\eta)

preserves
D(\mathcal{N}^{1/2})
the difference in (
4.2
) tends to
zero. Since
1-\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)^{*}\mathbb{W}_{u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}^{\Lambda^{\prime}}(\theta)
is uniformly bounded, we can
extend the convergence to arbitrary
\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, so

\mathbb{W}_{u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}^{\Lambda^{\prime}}(\theta)\to\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)
in the weak operator topology. Since
this is a family of unitary operators, this implies convergence in the
strong operator topology.

The fact that
\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)
is a Bogoliubov
transformation can be deduced from the property that
\tau_{u,\alpha}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\kappa_{u}^{\Lambda}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which holds for all
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
. A
proof of this well-known implication is provided in Appendix

B
(for a different proof see
e.g.
[
12
, Lem 4.8]
).

To demonstrate the final statement, we rely on the fact that
\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)
is a Bogoliubov transformation. This implies
the existence of bounded linear maps
\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}:L^{2}\oplus L^{2}\to L^{2}\oplus L^{2}
,
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathfrak{S}^{2}(L^{2}\oplus L^{2})
,
such that

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)^{*}c^{*}(f\oplus g)\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)=c^{*}(\mathfrak{u}(f\oplus g))+c(\mathfrak{v}\overline{(f\oplus g)})

for all
f,g\in L^{2}
, where
c^{*}(f\oplus g)=b^{*}(f)+a^{*}(g)
. Using this
relation, one can deduce (see
[
12
, Lem. 4.4]
) that for all

\ell\in\mathbb{N}

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)^{*}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}\,\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\leq\ell^{\ell}(1+2\|\mathfrak{v}\|_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}+\|\mathfrak{u}\|)^{\ell}\,(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}

as quadratic forms on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
. To bound the norms on the
right-hand side, we rely on the fact that
\|\mathfrak{v}\|_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}^{2}=\|\mathcal{N}^{1/2}\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\Omega\|^{2}\leq e^{C(\|u\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}})}
, where the estimate for
\|\mathcal{N}^{1/2}\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\Omega\|^{2}
is obtained by
estimating the derivative w.r.t.
\theta
, using the bounds from Lemma

6.13
and then applying Grönwall’s
inequality. Together, this proves (
4.12a
). Now using the fact
that the inverse of a Bogoliubov transformation is again a Bogoliubov
transformation, the proof of (
4.12b
) can be carried out in a
similar fashion. In fact, it is easy to show that
\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)c^{*}(f\oplus g)\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)^{*}=c^{*}(\mathfrak{u}^{*}(f\oplus g))-c(\overline{\mathfrak{v}^{*}(\bar{f}\oplus\bar{g})})
and thus we can follow
the same steps as in the proof of (
4.12a
).
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
, and

\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
. Then for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in[0,1]
we
have

\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)D(\mathbb{\mathbb{T}}^{r})\subset D(\mathbb{\mathbb{T}}^{r})

where
\mathbb{T}=\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{b}(-\Delta)+\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega)
.

Proof.

For
u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and finite
\Lambda
, the coefficients of the
generator,
\tau_{u,\alpha}(x)
and
\kappa^{\Lambda}_{u^{\theta}}(k,x)
, are
elements of
H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
in
x
and compactly supported in
k
. The
commutator
[\mathbb{T},\mathbb{\mathbb{D}}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)]
is
thus
\mathbb{T}
-bounded. From this the claim follows from Grönwall’s
inequality and interpolation.
∎

The next lemma shows that the dressing transformation
W^{\rm D}(\theta)

introduced in (
3.1
) is
effectively described by the evolution
\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(\theta)

introduced in Proposition
4.3
. The proof of the
lemma follows a similar strategy as the proof of Theorem

4.2
.

Lemma 4.5
.

There exists a constant
C>0

such that for all
(u,\alpha)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
,
\chi\in\mathcal{\mathcal{F}}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
with
\|\chi\|=1
,
N\geq 1
and
|\theta|\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\big\|W^{\rm D}(\theta)X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi-X_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\big\|

\displaystyle\leq e^{C(\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}})}\|(1+\mathcal{N})^{\frac{3}{2}}\chi\|N^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

Let
\zeta(\theta)=X_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)}W^{\rm D}(\theta)X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi
with
\zeta(0)=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}\chi

and write the norm difference as

(4.18)

\displaystyle\big\|W^{\rm D}(\theta)X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi-X_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\big\|

\displaystyle=\|\zeta(\theta)-\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\|.

Observe that dropping the projection
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}
from the
norm increases its value, as
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}\leq N}\zeta(\theta)=\zeta(\theta)
. Using
i\partial_{\theta}\zeta(\theta)=D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\zeta(\theta)
, with
D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)
given by
(
3.3
), and Proposition

4.3
, we obtain

(4.19)

\tfrac{d}{d\theta}\|\zeta(\theta)-\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\|^{2}\\
=2\mathrm{Im}\big\langle\zeta(\theta)-\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi,\left(D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)-\mathbb{D}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\right)\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\big\rangle.

To bound the right-side, we employ Lemma

6.13
, which states the existence of a
constant
C
, such that for all
(u,\alpha)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
,
\phi,\chi\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\big|\langle\phi,(D_{u,\alpha}(\theta)-\mathbb{D}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(\theta))\chi\rangle\big|\leq C\|\phi\|\,\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{3/2}\chi\|N^{-1/2}.

With this at hand, the proof is readily finished, as

(4.21)

\tfrac{d}{d\theta}\|\zeta(\theta)-\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\|^{2}\\
\leq C\|\zeta(\theta)-\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\|\,\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{3/2}\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\|N^{-1/2},

so using that
\|\zeta(0)-\chi\|=\|\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{b}>N}\chi\|\leq N^{-3}\|\mathcal{N}^{3/2}\chi\|^{2}
and integrating leads to

(4.22)

\displaystyle\|\zeta(\theta)-\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\|\leq C\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{3/2}\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)\chi\|N^{-1/2}.

The desired result now follows from (
4.12a
).
∎

In the next proposition we make precise how the dressing
\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(1)
interpolates between the dressed and undressed
Bogoliubov evolutions. This is the analogous statement to Lemma

3.4
for the mean-field flows on the level of the
fluctuations. The proof of the proposition, along with the proof of the
existence of
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
(Theorem
1.4
),
is the subject of Section
5
(see
Proposition
5.3
).

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
, and
\mathbb{U}
,
\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}
,
\mathbb{W}^{\infty}
the evolutions introduced in Theorem
1.4

and Propositions
4.1
,
4.3
. For
every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}(t)\,\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(1)\,=\,\mathbb{W}_{\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}^{\infty}(1)\,\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t).

Observe that the actions of the mean-field flows, with respect to which we
are considering fluctuations, on both sides of the identity agree. Indeed,
on the left
(u,\alpha)
is first evolved to
\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)
, by the
definition of the generator
\mathbb{D}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(\theta)
, which is
taken as the initial reference state in the dressed Bogoliubov transformation

\mathbb{U}^{\rm D}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}
, whose generator includes the
evolution
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{D}}
. On the right side, the reference states
evolve according to
(u,\alpha)\mapsto\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)\mapsto\mathfrak{D}[1]\circ\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)
, which equals

\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{D}}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)
by Lemma

3.4
.

The next statement is the last step in the preparation for the proof of
Theorem
1.5
. It is an immediate consequence of
Proposition
4.6
and Propositions
4.1
,

4.3
.

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
be the unitary of Theorem
1.4
for

(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)
and
(u,\alpha)
as stated in
the hypothesis. For every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant

C(\ell)
so that for all
|t|\geq 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)^{*}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}\,\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)\leq e^{C(\ell)R(t)}\,(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}

as quadratic forms on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, where
R(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}(\|u_{s}\|_{H^{3}}^{10}+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}}^{10})ds
.

Proof.

Starting from the identity of
Proposition
4.6
and acting on both sides with

\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}(1)^{*}
, we can express
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
in terms of three Bogoliubov evolutions, for each of which
the corresponding bounds on number operators have been established in
(
4.12a
), (
4.12b
) and
Proposition
4.1
. Collecting the right constant, this gives

(4.23)

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)^{*}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}\,\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)\\
\leq e^{C(\ell)(R^{\rm D}(t)+\|u_{t}^{\rm D}\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\|\alpha_{t}^{\rm D}\|_{L^{2}}+\|u\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}})}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}

with
R^{\rm D}(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}\|u_{s}^{\rm D}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|\alpha^{\rm D}_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}ds
where
(u^{\rm D}_{t},\alpha^{\rm D}_{t})=\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)
. That the exponential factor is bounded by
e^{C(\ell)R(t)}

follows from (
3.35a
) and (
3.40
).
∎

4.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.5

We are now ready to prove our second main result.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
,
\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, as given by the
hypothesis. Let
\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)
be the Bogoliubov
approximation of the dressing transformation of Proposition

4.3
and
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
be the
Nelson–Bogoliubov dynamics given by Theorem
1.4
,
and
\mathbb{U}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}^{\rm D}(t)
be the dressed
Bogoliubov dynamics of Proposition
4.1
.

Using (
1.25
), we can write the norm
difference that we want to estimate in terms of the dressed dynamics,

(4.24)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}

\displaystyle=\big\|e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi-W^{\rm D}X^{*}_{\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi\big\|.

We split this into the difference of the dressed dynamics and its
Bogoliubov approximation, and the corresponding approximation of the
dressing transformation, i.e., we estimate
\mathscr{D}\leq\mathscr{D}_{1}+\mathscr{D}_{2}+\mathscr{D}_{3}
with

(4.25a)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{1}

\displaystyle=\big\|e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}W^{\rm D}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi-e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}X_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)\chi\big\|

(4.25b)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|e^{-itH_{N}^{\rm D}}X_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)\chi-X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{D}}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{U}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}^{\rm D}(t)\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)\chi\big\|

(4.25c)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{3}

\displaystyle=\big\|X_{\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{D}}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{U}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}^{\rm D}(t)\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)\chi-W^{\rm D}X_{\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi\big\|.

Recalling
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{D}[1]
and applying Lemma

4.5
with
\theta=1
immediately gives

(4.26)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{1}=\big\|W^{\rm D}X_{u,\alpha}^{*}\chi-X_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)\chi\big\|\leq C\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{3/2}\chi\|N^{-1/2}.

By Proposition
4.6
and Lemma

3.4
, we have

(4.27)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{3}

\displaystyle=\big\|X_{\mathfrak{D}\circ\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}(1)\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi-W^{\rm D}X_{\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)}^{*}\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi\big\|,

and applying Lemma
4.5
together with
Proposition
2.2
then yields (here
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)
)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{3}

\displaystyle\leq e^{C(\|u_{t}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha_{t}\|_{L^{2}})}\big\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{3/2}\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi\big\|N^{-1/2}

(4.28)

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{3/2}\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)\chi\big\|N^{-1/2}.

Using Corollary
4.7
we arrive at the desired
bound
\mathscr{D}_{3}\leq e^{CR(t)}\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{3/2}\chi\|N^{-1/2}
. To estimate
\mathscr{D}_{2}
, we apply Theorem

4.2
(note that

\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
by Lemma

3.3
and
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
) so that

(4.29)

\mathscr{D}_{2}\leq e^{CR^{\rm D}(t)}\delta^{\rm D}(\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)\chi)^{1/2}\sqrt{\ln N}N^{-1/4}

with
R^{\rm D}(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}\|u^{\rm D}_{s}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|\alpha^{\rm D}_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}ds
and
(u_{t}^{\mathrm{D}},\alpha_{t}^{\mathrm{D}})=\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{D}}[t]\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha)=\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha))
.
In view of the bound on
R^{\rm D}(t)
stated in (
3.40
),
this yields the claimed bound with

(4.30)

\delta(\chi)=\delta^{\mathrm{D}}(\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)\chi).

The domain of
\delta
is dense in
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
since it is the image
under the continuous map
\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)^{*}
of the dense
set
D((\mathcal{N}^{3}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2})
. The set
D((\mathcal{N}^{3}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{F}_{\perp u^{\mathrm{D}}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
is also dense in
\mathcal{F}_{\perp u^{\mathrm{D}}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, as
\Gamma(q_{u^{\mathrm{D}}})
is continuous
and leaves
D((\mathcal{N}^{3}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2})
invariant for
u^{\mathrm{D}}\in H^{1}
. By the mapping property from
Proposition
4.3
, we have
\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\infty}(1)^{*}\mathcal{F}_{\perp u^{\mathrm{D}}}\otimes\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, so the image of
D((\mathcal{N}^{3}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2})
is also
dense in
\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F}
. This completes the proof.
∎

5.
Renormalization of the Nelson–Bogoliubov evolution

This section is dedicated to proving the existence of the renormalized
Nelson–Bogoliubov evolution, which is stated in Theorem

1.4
. To accomplish this, we consider a family of
Bogoliubov Hamiltonians
\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha,\theta}^{\Lambda}
that
interpolate between
\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}
and

\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\mathrm{D}}
with “dressing parameter”

\theta\in[0,1]
and UV cutoff

\Lambda<\infty
. The key point is that for
\theta=1
we can remove the cutoff, i.e.,

\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\infty}=\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\mathrm{D}}
, and that
the Bogoliubov transformations
\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta)

associated with the dressing interpolate between the different members of

(\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha,\theta}^{\Lambda})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
. We exploit this
property to prove Theorem
1.4
in
Section
5.2
by defining
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
as the
Bogoliubov transformation generated by
\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\mathrm{D}}
and
transformed by the Bogoliubov approximation of the dressing. Along with the
existence of
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
, this also proves Proposition

4.6
.

Even though the general strategy in this section is motivated by Nelson’s
original approach for renormalizing the Nelson Hamiltonian, the argument is
more involved on the level of the fluctuations. This is because the dressing
transformation and the different Nelson–Bogoliubov evolutions are all
generated by non-autonomous equations.

5.1.
Bogoliubov Hamiltonians with
\theta\in[0,1]

In order to define the Bogoliubov Hamiltonians

\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(t)
, we first introduce the partially dressed
mean-field flow

(5.1)

\mathfrak{s}_{\theta}[t]=\mathfrak{D}[\theta]\circ\mathfrak{s}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}[-\theta],

where
\mathfrak{s}
is the flow of the Schrödinger–Klein–Gordon
system (
2.21
). For the next
statement recall the definitions below (
3.3
) and
\phi_{\alpha}(x)=2\mathrm{Re}\big\langle G_{x},\alpha\big\rangle
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For
(u,\alpha)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}
, the function

(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}_{\theta}[t](u,\alpha)
is the unique solution
to the Hamiltonian equations

(5.2)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} i\partial_{t}u_{t}(x)&=h_{u_{t},\alpha_{t},\theta}u_{t}(x)\\[4.2679pt]
i\partial_{t}\alpha_{t}(k)&=\omega(k)\alpha_{t}+(1-\theta)\langle u_{t},G_{(\cdot)}(k)u_{t}\rangle\\[2.84526pt]
&\qquad+2\theta\langle u_{t},kB_{(\cdot)}(k)(-i\nabla+\theta F_{\alpha_{t}})u_{t}\rangle\end{aligned}\end{cases}

with conserved energy

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\theta}(u,\alpha)

\displaystyle\quad=\big\langle u,\big(-\Delta+(1-\theta)\phi_{\alpha}+\theta A_{\alpha}+\theta^{2}(F_{\alpha})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}V_{\theta}*\left|u\right|^{2}\big)u\big\rangle+\big\langle\alpha,\omega\alpha\big\rangle,

where

(5.4a)

\displaystyle h_{u,\alpha,\theta}

\displaystyle=-\Delta+(1-\theta)\phi_{\alpha}+\theta A_{\alpha}+\theta^{2}(F_{\alpha})^{2}+V_{\theta}\ast|u|^{2}-\mu_{u,\alpha,\theta}

(5.4b)

\displaystyle\mu_{u,\alpha,\theta}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1-\theta}{2}\big\langle u,\phi_{\alpha}u\big\rangle+\tfrac{1}{2}\big\langle u,V_{\theta}\ast|u|^{2}u\big\rangle+\theta\mathrm{Re}\big\langle\alpha,f_{u}+\theta g_{u,\alpha}\big\rangle

(5.4c)

\displaystyle V_{\theta}(x)

\displaystyle=-4\theta\mathrm{Re}\big\langle G_{x},B_{0}\big\rangle+2\theta^{2}\mathrm{Re}\big\langle B_{x},\omega B_{0}\big\rangle.

Remark 5.1
.

Since, for
\theta=1
,
Eqs. (
5.2
) coincide with the dressed
mean-field equations (
3.3
), we have
s_{1}[t]=\mathfrak{s}^{\rm D}[t]
. Lemma
5.1
thus provides a proof
of Lemma
3.4
.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

One checks by direct calculation
[
2
, Prop.III.12]
that

(5.5)

\mathcal{E}_{\theta}=\mathcal{E}_{0}\circ\mathfrak{D}[-\theta].

Since
\mathfrak{s}
is a Hamiltonian flow of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
with respect
to the symplectic form

(5.6)

\sigma((u,\alpha),(u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}))=2\mathrm{Im}(\langle u,u^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}}),

and the equations (
5.2
) are the
Hamiltonian equations for
\mathcal{E}_{\theta}
, the claim should follow by
showing that
\mathfrak{D}
acts by symplectic transformations. However,
since the involved spaces are infinite-dimensional, we need to take care of
some domain issues. These are addressed by
[
26
, Lem.6.9]
, so we
will check the hypothesis of this Lemma (the reader might find it helpful
to consult the examples given in
[
26
, Sect.6.5]
).

(1)

(u,\alpha)\mapsto\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)
must be
differentiable with derivative continuous on

E=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}
, and symplectic. The derivative of
the flow is

(5.7)

\partial_{(v,\eta)}\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)=\Big(e^{-i\theta\tau_{u,\alpha}}v-i\theta(\tilde{\phi}_{\eta}-2\mathrm{Re}\langle u,\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha}v\rangle)u^{\theta},\gamma+\theta B_{0}2\widehat{\mathrm{Re}\bar{u}v}\Big).

As a linear function of
(v,\eta)
, this is continuous on

H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}
, for all
(u,\alpha)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}
, as follows from the bounds of
Lemma
6.3
. Using the
formula (
5.7
), one also checks that

(5.8)

\displaystyle\sigma(\partial_{(v,\eta)}\mathfrak{D},\partial_{(v^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})}\mathfrak{D})=\sigma((v,\eta),(v^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}),

by using that
B_{0}
is an even function and observing that the mixed
terms in
v,\eta^{\prime}
cancel each other (see
also
[
2
, Prop. IV.1]
), i.e.,
\mathfrak{D}
is symplectic in
the sense of
[
26
, Def.6.5]
.

(2)

The domain
\mathscr{D}
on which the Hamiltonian vector field (given
by Equation (
5.2
)) is defined must
be invariant under the flow
\mathfrak{D}
. We take

\mathscr{D}=H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
, so this follows from
Lemma
3.3
.

(3)

The derivative of
\mathcal{E}_{\theta}
on
\mathscr{D}
should be
compatible with the symplectic structure as
in
[
26
, Def. 6.5]
. Since in our case the symplectic map is simply

\mathscr{J}=2i
with range
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}\subset E^{\prime}
, we must check that for
(u,\alpha)\in\mathscr{D}
, the right hand
side of Equation (
5.2
) is an element
of
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}
. This follows from the bounds of
Lemmas
6.3
and

6.4
.

Thus the hypothesis of
[
26
, Lem. 6.9]
are satisfied and this
implies the claim.
∎

Next, we introduce a family of
\theta
- and
t
-dependent quadratic
operators
\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha,\theta}^{\Lambda}(\theta)
on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}

that is associated with the mean-field flow
\mathfrak{s}_{\theta}[t](u,\alpha)
and defined such that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha,0}^{\Lambda}(t)=\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(t)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\infty}(t)=\mathbb{H}^{\rm D}_{u,\alpha}(t)

for the Nelson–Bogoliubov Hamiltonian (
1.22
) and the
dressed Bogoliubov Hamiltonian (
4.1
). These are
essentially the Bogoliubov Hamiltonians associated with the partially dressed
Hamiltonians
W^{\mathrm{D}}(\theta)H_{N}W^{\mathrm{D}}(\theta)^{*}
. It is
important to note that for
\theta\in[0,1)
the operator needs to be defined
with a UV cutoff
\Lambda<\infty
, while for
\theta=1
the definition
makes sense also for
\Lambda=\infty
.

Now more concretely, for
(u,\alpha)
and
\theta\in[0,1]
, let
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}_{\theta}[t](u,\alpha)
denote the solution to
(
5.2
), and define

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha,\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)=\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{b}(h_{u_{t},\alpha_{t},\theta})+\mathbb{K}^{(1),\Lambda}_{\theta,u_{t}}+\big(\mathbb{K}^{(2),\Lambda}_{\theta,u_{t}}+\text{h.c.}\big)+\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega)

\displaystyle\quad+\int dxdk\,\Big(\big(q_{u_{t}}L^{\Lambda}_{\theta,\alpha_{t}}(k)u_{t}\big)(x)a^{*}_{k}b_{x}^{*}+\big(q_{u_{t}}L^{\Lambda}_{\theta,\alpha_{t}}(k)^{*}u_{t}\big)(x)a_{k}b_{x}^{*}\Big)+\text{h.c.}

\displaystyle\quad+\theta^{2}\int dkdl\,\Big(-2M_{u_{t}}^{\Lambda}(k,-l)a^{*}_{k}a_{l}+M^{\Lambda}_{u_{t}}(k,l)a_{k}^{*}a_{l}^{*}+M^{\Lambda}_{u_{t}}(-k,-l)a_{k}a_{l}\Big)

with
h_{u_{t},\alpha_{t},\theta}
defined in (
5.4a
),

(5.11a)

\displaystyle\big(L^{\Lambda}_{\theta,\alpha}(k)u\big)(x)

\displaystyle=(1-\theta)G^{\Lambda}_{x}(k)+2B^{\Lambda}_{x}(k)k\big((-i\theta\nabla+\theta^{2}F_{\alpha}(x))u\big)(x)

(5.11b)

\displaystyle M^{\Lambda}_{u}(k,l)

\displaystyle=\big\langle u,kB_{(\cdot)}^{\Lambda}(k)\cdot lB_{(\cdot)}^{\Lambda}(l)u\big\rangle,

and

(5.11c)

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}^{(1),\Lambda}_{\theta,u}

\displaystyle=\int dxdy\,K_{\theta,u}^{(1),\Lambda}(x,y)b^{*}_{x}b_{y},\quad\mathbb{K}^{(2),\Lambda}_{\theta,u}=\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\,K_{\theta,u}^{(2),\Lambda}(x,y)b^{*}_{x}b^{*}_{y}

with

(5.11d)

\displaystyle K_{\theta,u}^{(1),\Lambda}=q_{u}\widetilde{K}^{(1),\Lambda}_{\theta,u}q_{u},\qquad\widetilde{K}_{\theta,u}^{(1),\Lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=u(x)V_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(x-y)\overline{u(y)},

(5.11e)

\displaystyle K_{\theta,u}^{(2),\Lambda}=(q_{u}\otimes q_{u})\widetilde{K}^{(2),\Lambda}_{\theta,u},\qquad\widetilde{K}_{\theta,u}^{(2),\Lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=u(x)V_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(x-y)u(y),

where
V_{\theta}
is defined in (
5.4c
). For
\theta=1
and

\Lambda=\infty
these definitions coincide with those from Section

4.1
. For
\theta\neq 1
, the cutoff

\Lambda<\infty
is necessary, since the term involving
G_{x}^{\Lambda}(k)
in
(
5.11a
) does not yield a quadratic form on

D(\mathbb{T}^{1/2})
for
\Lambda=\infty
. All other terms are in fact
unproblematic for
\Lambda=\infty
also if
\theta\neq 1
. For the purpose
of the proof of Proposition
5.3
, where cancellations
between different terms are important, we work with the definition given
above.

The next proposition states the existence and suitable properties of the
unitary time evolution generated by
\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,\theta}(t)
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
and let
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}_{\theta}[t](u,\alpha)
be given by (
5.1
). Moreover,
let
\theta=1
and
\Lambda=\infty
or
\theta\neq 1
and
\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
. For every
\Psi\in D((\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2})

there exists a unique solution to the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} i\partial_{t}\Psi(t)&=\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,\theta}(t)\Psi(t)\\
\Psi(0)&=\Psi_{0}\end{aligned}\end{cases}

such that
\Psi\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F})\cap L_{\rm{\mathrm{loc}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},D((\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2}))
. The solution map
\Psi_{0}\mapsto\Psi(t)
extends to a unitary
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)
on

\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
satisfying
\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,\theta}(t)(\mathcal{F}_{\perp u}\otimes\mathcal{F})\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\perp u_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{F}
. Moreover, for
\theta=1
we have the following properties.

(i)

There is a constant
C>0
such that for all

\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}(t)^{*}(\mathbb{T}+\mathcal{N})\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}(t)\leq e^{CR^{\rm D}(t)}(\mathbb{T}+\mathcal{N}+1)

in the sense of quadratic forms on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, where
R^{\rm D}(t)=1+\int_{0}^{|t|}\|u_{s}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}ds
.

(ii)

For every
t\in\mathbb{R}

\mathbb{U}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha,1}(t)=\slim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,1}(t).

(iii)

\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,1}(t)
is a
Bogoliubov transformation for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
.

(iv)

For every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there is a constant

C(\ell)
such that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}(t)^{*}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}\,\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}(t)\leq e^{C(\ell)R^{\rm D}(t)}\,(\mathcal{N}+1)^{\ell}

in the sense of quadratic forms on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, with
R^{\rm D}(t)

as in
(i)
.

Proof.

Existence and uniqueness of the dynamics and Property (i) follow
from
[
66
, Thm.8]
and the bounds of
Lemma
6.8
. Note that the existence of the
unitary
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)
follows by the same
reasoning as in the proof of Proposition
4.3
and
that the mapping property is obtained by a similar argument as in
(
4.13
).

To prove (ii) use Duhamel’s formula for
\Psi,\Xi\in D((\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{\mathbb{T}})^{1/2})
together with Lemma

6.9
to obtain

(5.12)

\displaystyle|\langle\Xi,(1-\mathbb{U}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha,1}(t)^{*}\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,1}(t))\Psi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{|t|}ds\,|\langle\Xi,\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\infty}(s)^{*}(\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\infty}(s)-\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}(s))\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,1}(s)\Psi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{\Lambda}\int_{0}^{|t|}ds\,e^{CR^{\rm D}(s)}\|(\mathbb{T}+\mathcal{N}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\infty}(s)\Xi\|\,\|(\mathbb{T}+\mathcal{N}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,1}(s)\Psi\|

where
R^{\rm D}(s)=\|u_{s}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|\alpha_{s}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{3/2}})^{2}
and
\varepsilon_{\Lambda}\to 0
as
\Lambda\to 0
. The
right hand side thus converges to zero by Property (i). This implies strong
convergence of
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}
to

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\infty}
by unitarity of

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}
,
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
, as
argued in the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

In Appendix
B
, we provide a proof that

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}(t)
is a Bogoliubov transformation. For
finite
\Lambda
, this is essentially due to the square-integrability of
the kernels in (
5.10
) appearing in the terms with
b^{*}b^{*}
,
b^{*}a^{*}
and
a^{*}a^{*}
. For
\Lambda=\infty
, however, the kernel
corresponding to
a^{*}b^{*}
fails to meet the Hilbert–Schmidt criterion. In
this case, we establish the statement by proving a suitable approximation
argument and utilizing the fact that
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}(t)\to\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\infty}(t)
converges strongly.

The final statement can be derived using the same logic as in the proof of
Proposition
4.3
, in combination with
Proposition
5.2
(i).
∎

5.2.
Dressing identity and proof of
Theorem
1.4

We can now prepare for the proof of Theorem
1.4
. We
will start by making precise how the unitaries
\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)
interpolate between the Bogoliubov dynamics

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)
for different
\theta
. For

\theta=1
we can then take the limit
\Lambda\to\infty
of

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,1}^{\Lambda}(t)
to obtain information on the behavior of

\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,0}(t)
as
\Lambda\to\infty
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
, and consider the evolutions

(5.13)

\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha),\theta}(t)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{W}_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha)}^{\Lambda}(\theta),

with generators
\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha),\theta}(t)
and
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha)}^{\Lambda}(\theta)
, respectively (see
Propositions
5.2
and
4.3
). It is
important to keep in mind that the subscripts refer to the initial
condition of the mean-field flow, which is used to define the generator of
each evolution.

The next result shows that the two flows in
(
5.13
) commute, up to a global phase. The
additional phase is due to the fact that we wrote
\mathbb{H}_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha),\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)
in normal order, which is not
preserved by the transformations. As discussed below, this identity is the
key ingredient for the renormalization of the Nelson–Bogoliubov Hamiltonian.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
(u,\alpha)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{5/2}
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
. If we denote

E^{\Lambda}_{\theta}=(2\theta-\theta^{2})\langle G^{\Lambda}_{0},B^{\Lambda}_{0}\rangle+\tfrac{1}{2}\langle u,V_{\theta}^{\Lambda}\ast|u|^{2}u\rangle

then for all
t,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
we
have the identity

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,0}(t)e^{-itE^{\Lambda}_{\theta}}=\mathbb{W}_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha)}^{\Lambda}(\theta)^{*}\,\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha),\theta}(t)\,\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(\theta).

This proposition follows from the uniqueness of both sides by comparing their
derivatives. The lengthy calculation is given in
Section
5.3
. Assuming this for now,
we can prove Theorem
1.4
and Proposition

4.6
.

Proofs of Theorem
1.4
and Proposition
4.6
.

By strong continuity of
\mathbb{W}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(\theta)
in
\Lambda

(Lemma
4.3
) and of

\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,1}(t)
(Proposition
5.2
), we
have

(5.14)

\slim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\mathbb{W}_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha)}^{\Lambda}(1)^{*}\,\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha),1}(t)\,\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha}(0)\\
=\mathbb{W}_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha)}^{\infty}(1)^{*}\,\mathbb{U}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha),1}(t)\,\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(0)

By Proposition
5.3
with
\theta=1
and
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}^{\Lambda}(t)=\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,0}^{\Lambda}(t)
this shows
that

(5.15)

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)=\slim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,0}(t)e^{-itE^{\Lambda}_{1}}=\mathbb{W}_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha)}^{\infty}(1)^{*}\,\mathbb{U}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha),1}(t)\,\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(0).

Strong continuity of
t\mapsto\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
follows since the
right hand side is strongly continuous, as for
\Psi\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}

the maps

(5.16)

t\mapsto\mathbb{W}_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha)}^{\infty}(1)^{*}\Psi,\,\qquad t\mapsto\mathbb{U}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{D}(u,\alpha),1}(t)\Psi\,\qquad t\mapsto\mathbb{W}^{\infty}_{u,\alpha}(0)\Psi.

are continuous.

To show the mapping property for
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
, consider

\Gamma(q_{u_{t}})
as in the proof of Proposition

4.3
for
(u_{t},\alpha_{t})=\mathfrak{s}[t](u,\alpha)
. By the same argument as in
(
4.13
) one shows that
\|\Gamma(q_{u_{t}})\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{u,\alpha,0}(t)\Psi\|=0
for every
\Psi\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
and all
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
. The
desired result then follows from
\|\Gamma(q_{u_{t}})\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha,0}^{\Lambda}(t)\Psi\|\rightarrow\|\Gamma(q_{u_{t}})\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)\Psi\|^{2}
as
\Lambda\to\infty
.

The property that
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
is a Bogoliubov
transformation is a direct consequence of the result that it is a
composition of three Bogoliubov transformations.
∎

Remark 5.2

(On the renormalized Nelson–Bogoliubov Hamiltonian)
.

If the evolution
\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
was a semi-group, we could
deduce from Theorem
1.4
the existence of a
generator
\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}
that would represent the renormalization
of the Nelson–Bogoliubov Hamiltonian (
1.22
). But

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
is associated with a non-autonomous evolution
equation and
\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}
should depend on the time
t
. In this
setting, only the following, weaker theory is available
(see
[
81
]
for details). Consider the extension of

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
to a two-parameter family

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t,s)
with

\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t,0)=\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(t)
. On the Banach space

C_{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F})
of continuous
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
-valued
functions tending to zero at infinity, we can define the corresponding
evolution semi-group of isometries by

(5.17)

(T(t)\chi)(s)=\mathbb{U}_{u,\alpha}(s,s-t)\chi(s-t).

Then
T
has a generator
A
,
D(A)\subset C_{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F})
,
which corresponds formally to

(5.18)

(A\chi)(s)=\tfrac{d}{dt}T(t)\big|_{t=0}\chi(s)=\big(-i\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}(s)-\tfrac{d}{ds}\big)\chi(s).

However, we do not have any information concerning the domain or
self-adjointness of
\mathbb{H}_{u,\alpha}(t)
(one could consider
using
[
81
, Thm. 2.9]
, but this does not apply since the
solutions provided by Proposition
5.2
need not be
differentiable in the norm of
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
).

5.3.
Proof of the dressing identity

In this section, we derive the dressing identity for the Nelson–Bogoliubov
evolution, that is, we prove Proposition
5.3
. To enhance
the clarity of the presentation, we will use the shorthand notation

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha),\theta}(t)\quad\qquad

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta):=\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha)}(\theta)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha),\theta}(t)\qquad

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\Lambda}_{t}(\theta):=\mathbb{D}^{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha)}(\theta)\qquad

\displaystyle.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Adopting
(
5.19
), the identity we aim to prove becomes
\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{0}(t)e^{-itE^{\Lambda}_{1}}=\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)^{*}\,\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t)\,\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{0}(\theta)
. The idea is to prove a differential version of this
identity after taking derivatives in
t
and
\theta
. To make this
precise, first note that the coefficients of

\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)
given in (
4.10
)
depend on
u,\alpha
in a differentiable way. Since for
(u,\alpha)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{1}
the flow
\mathfrak{s}_{\theta}[t](u,\alpha)
is differentiable in
t
in the
L^{2}
-sense, this implies that for
any
\Psi\in D(\mathcal{N})
the mapping
t\mapsto\mathbb{D}^{\Lambda}_{t}(\theta)\Psi
is differentiable in
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
. Since
\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)
preserves
D(\mathcal{N})
, we deduce from Duhamel’s
formula (
4.2
) that
\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)\Psi
is
differentiable in
t
for
\Psi\in D(\mathcal{N})
and

(5.20)

\big(i\partial_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)^{*}\big)\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)\Psi=-\int_{0}^{\theta}d\eta\,\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\eta)^{*}\big(\partial_{t}\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}\big)(\eta)\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\eta)\Psi.

Denote by
\mathbb{V}_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(t):=\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)^{*}\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t)\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{0}(\theta)
the right
hand side of the identity we want to prove. It follows from our previous
considerations and Lemma
4.4
that for
\Psi\in D((\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2})
we have

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\mathbb{V}_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)\Psi

\displaystyle=\big(i\partial_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)^{*}\big)\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t)\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{0}(\theta)\Psi+\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)^{*}\mathbb{H}_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)\mathbb{U}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t)\mathbb{W}^{\Lambda}_{0}(\theta)\Psi

(5.21)

\displaystyle=:\mathbb{B}_{\theta}(t)\mathbb{V}_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)\Psi

in
D((\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T})^{-1/2})
. By uniqueness of the solutions
proved in Proposition
5.2
our claim will follow if we can
show that the generators of
\mathbb{U}_{0}^{\Lambda}(t)e^{-iE_{\theta}^{\Lambda}t}
and
\mathbb{V}_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)
are equal, that is for every

t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\Lambda}(t)+E_{\theta}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{B}_{\theta}(t)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\theta}d\eta\,\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\eta)^{*}\big(\partial_{t}\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}\big)(\eta)\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\eta)+\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)^{*}\mathbb{H}_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta).

Equality holds for
\theta=0
since
E_{0}^{\Lambda}=0
and
\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(0)=1
, so it is sufficient to prove that for all
\Psi,\Xi\in D((\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2})

(5.23)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=i\partial_{\theta}\Big\langle\Xi,\Big(\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\Lambda}(t)+E_{\theta}^{\Lambda}-\mathbb{B}_{\theta}(t)\Big)\Psi\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)\Xi,\Big(i\partial_{\theta}E_{\theta}^{\Lambda}+i\partial_{t}\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)-i\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{H}_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(t)-[\mathbb{H}_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(t),\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)]\Big)\mathbb{W}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)\Psi\Big\rangle.

where we anticipated differentiability of
\mathbb{H}_{\theta}^{\Lambda}
which
follows easily from the explicit calculation of its derivative below.

The remainder of the proof is an explicit calculation of this quadratic
form.
For ease of presentation, we set, using that the flows commute,

(5.24)

(u_{t}^{\theta},\alpha_{t}^{\theta}):=\mathfrak{s}_{\theta}[t]\circ\mathfrak{D}[\theta](u,\alpha)=\mathfrak{D}[\theta]\circ\mathfrak{s}_{0}[t](u,\alpha).

Moreover, we do not make the dependence of the different objects on

t,\theta,\Lambda
explicit everywhere and adopt the following shorthand
notation

(5.25a)

\displaystyle h_{\theta}

\displaystyle=h_{u_{t}^{\theta},\alpha_{t}^{\theta},\theta}

(5.25b)

\displaystyle\tau_{t}

\displaystyle=\tau_{u_{t},\alpha_{t}}

(5.25c)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=q_{u^{\theta}_{t}}=1-|u_{t}\rangle\langle u^{\theta}_{t}|

(5.25d)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,x)

\displaystyle=\big(qiB_{(\cdot)}^{\Lambda}(k)u_{t}^{\theta}\big)(x)

(5.25e)

\displaystyle L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)

\displaystyle=(1-\theta)G^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}+2B^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)k(-i\theta\nabla+\theta^{2}F_{\alpha_{t}^{\theta}}(\cdot))

(5.25f)

\displaystyle M^{\Lambda}(k,l)

\displaystyle=\langle u^{\theta}_{t},kB^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)lB^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(l)u^{\theta}_{t}\rangle

(5.25g)

\displaystyle K^{(1),\Lambda}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=K^{(1),\Lambda}_{\theta,u_{t}^{\theta}}

(5.25h)

\displaystyle K^{(2),\Lambda}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=K^{(2),\Lambda}_{\theta,u_{t}^{\theta}}

(5.25i)

\displaystyle V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(x)

\displaystyle=-4\theta\mathrm{Re}\big\langle G^{\Lambda}_{x},B_{0}\big\rangle+2\theta^{2}\mathrm{Re}\big\langle B_{x}^{\Lambda},\omega B_{0}\big\rangle

Note that
M^{\Lambda}(k,l)
is independent of
\theta
as the flow

\mathfrak{D}
preserves the modulus of
u
.

After commuting
\mathbb{H}_{\theta}
and
\mathbb{D}_{t}
, the expression
from (
5.23
) takes the form

\displaystyle-i\partial_{t}\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)+i\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t)+[\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t),\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)]

(5.26a)

\displaystyle\ =\int dx\,b_{x}^{*}\big(-i\partial_{t}\tau_{t}+i\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}+[h_{\theta},\tau_{t}]\big)b_{x}\,

(5.26b)

\displaystyle\quad+\int dxdy\,D(x,y)b_{x}^{*}b_{y}+iE_{b}+\bigg(\int dxdy\,\widetilde{D}(x,y)\,b_{x}^{*}b_{y}^{*}-\text{h.c.}\bigg)

(5.26c)

\displaystyle\quad+\int dkdx\Big(X(k,x)\,a_{k}^{*}b_{x}^{*}+\widetilde{X}(k,x)\,a_{k}b_{x}^{*}\Big)-\text{h.c.}

(5.26d)

\displaystyle\quad+\int dkdl\,A(k,l)a_{k}^{*}a_{l}+iE_{a}+\bigg(\int dkdl\,\widetilde{A}(k,l)a_{k}^{*}a_{l}^{*}-\text{h.c.}\bigg),

in the sense of sesquilinear forms on
D((\mathcal{N}+\mathbb{T})^{1/2})
. We will now show that all the coefficients of
creation and annihilation operators vanish, and that
E_{a}+E_{b}=\partial_{\theta}E^{\Lambda}_{\theta}
.

Mean-field part (
5.26a
)
: By
(
3.15
), Lemma

5.1
and (
5.24
) we have

\partial_{\theta}u^{\theta}_{t}=-i\tau_{t}u^{\theta}_{t}
and
\partial_{t}u_{t}^{\theta}=-ih_{\theta}u_{t}^{\theta}
, and since the derivatives commute

(5.27)

\displaystyle 0=(\partial_{t}\partial_{\theta}-\partial_{\theta}\partial_{t})u_{t}^{\theta}=\big(-(i\partial_{t}\tau_{t})+i(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta})+[h_{\theta},\tau_{t}]\big)u_{t}^{\theta}.

Since (
5.24
) is a bijection, this implies that
(
5.26a
) vanishes.

Terms quadratic in
b,b^{*}
and

E_{b}
(
5.26b
)
: In the commutator
[\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t),\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}(\theta)]
a quadratic term in

b,b^{*}
can arise either by commuting two terms with
b^{\#}a^{\#}
, or terms
with
b^{\#}b^{\#}
. In the first case, the coefficients are combinations of

L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)
and
\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,\cdot)
integrated over

k
. In the latter case, some terms have already been taken into account
in (
5.26a
) and only the commutators of

K^{(1),\Lambda}_{\theta}
,
K^{(2),\Lambda}_{\theta}
with
\tau_{t}

remain. Combining these with the derivatives of
K^{(1),\Lambda}_{\theta}
,

K_{\theta}^{(2),\Lambda}
and putting them in normal order yields

\displaystyle D(x,y)

\displaystyle=i\partial_{\theta}K^{\Lambda,(1)}_{\theta}(x,y)-\big(\tau_{t}(x)-\tau_{t}(y)\big)K^{\Lambda,(1)}_{\theta}(x,y)

\displaystyle\quad-\int dk\,\big(q(L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)+L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(-k)^{*})u^{\theta}_{t}\big)(x)\overline{\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,y)}

(5.28a)

\displaystyle\quad+\int dk\,\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,x)\overline{\big(q(L_{\theta}^{\Lambda}(k)+L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(-k)^{*})u^{\theta}_{t}\big)(y)},

(5.28b)

\displaystyle E_{b}

\displaystyle=i\int dxdk\,\overline{\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,x)}\big(q(L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)+L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(-k)^{*})\big)u^{\theta}_{t}(x),

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\theta}K_{\theta}^{(2),\Lambda}(x,y)-\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{t}(x)+\tau_{t}(y))K_{\theta}^{(2),\Lambda}(x,y)

(5.28c)

\displaystyle\quad+\int dk\,\big(q(L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)^{*}+L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(-k))u^{\theta}_{t}\big)(x)\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,y).

We now show that
D(x,y)=0
. First, we may observe that
i\partial_{\theta}u^{\theta}_{t}=\tau_{t}u^{\theta}_{t}
and
q=1-|u^{\theta}_{t}\rangle\langle u^{\theta}_{t}|
imply for any operator
T_{\theta}
the identity

(5.29)

i\partial_{\theta}(qT_{\theta}u^{\theta}_{t})=\tau_{t}qT_{\theta}u^{\theta}_{t}+q[T_{\theta},\tau_{t}]u^{\theta}_{t}+q(i\partial_{\theta}T_{\theta})u^{\theta}_{t}.

Since
[V^{\Lambda}_{\theta},\tau_{t}]=0
, this gives us

\displaystyle i\partial_{\theta}K^{(1),\Lambda}_{\theta}(x,y)-\big(\tau_{t}(x)-\tau_{t}(y)\big)K^{(1),\Lambda}_{\theta}(x,y)

(5.30)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int dzdz^{\prime}q(x,z)u^{\theta}_{t}(z)(i\partial_{\theta}V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(z-z^{\prime}))\bar{u}^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime})q(z^{\prime},y).

To evaluate the terms involving

L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)+L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(-k)^{*}
, we first calculate using

\overline{B^{\Lambda}_{x}}(-k)=B^{\Lambda}_{x}(k)

(5.31)

\displaystyle(L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)+L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(-k)^{*})u^{\theta}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\big((1-\theta)2G^{\Lambda}_{x}(k)+2\theta k^{2}B^{\Lambda}_{x}(k)\big)u^{\theta}_{t}(x).

This gives

(5.32)

\displaystyle-\int dk\,\big(q(L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)+L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(-k)^{*})u\big)(x)\overline{\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,y)}

\displaystyle=\int dzdz^{\prime}q(x,z)u^{\theta}_{t}(z)\Big(2i(1-\theta)\langle B^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}},G^{\Lambda}_{z}\rangle+2i\theta\langle k^{2}B^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}},B^{\Lambda}_{z}\rangle\Big)\bar{u}^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime})q(z^{\prime},y).

Adding this and minus its complex conjugate with
x,y
exchanged (which
leads to an exchange of
z,z^{\prime}
) gives with
(k^{2}+\omega(k))B_{x}(k)=G_{x}(k)

(5.33)

\displaystyle\int\hskip-6.0ptdzdz^{\prime}q(x,z)u^{\theta}_{t}(z)\big(\underbrace{4i(1-\theta)\mathrm{Re}\langle B^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}},G^{\Lambda}_{z}\rangle+4i\theta\mathrm{Re}\langle k^{2}B^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}},B^{\Lambda}_{z}\rangle}_{=4i\mathrm{Re}\langle B^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}},G^{\Lambda}_{z}\rangle-4\theta i\mathrm{Re}\langle\omega B^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}},B^{\Lambda}_{z}\rangle=-i\partial_{\theta}V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}}\big)\bar{u}^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime})q(z^{\prime},y).

Combined with (
5.3
), this shows that
D\equiv 0
.

For
\widetilde{D}
this follows from the same calculation, using
that
\langle B_{x}^{\Lambda},G_{y}^{\Lambda}\rangle=\widehat{B_{0}^{\Lambda}G_{0}^{\Lambda}}(x-y)
is real-valued since
G,B
are even functions. By the
same reasoning, the value of the constant is

\displaystyle E_{b}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} -i\int dxdzdz^{\prime}q(x,z)u^{\theta}_{t}(z)&\Big(2i(1-\theta)\langle B^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}},G^{\Lambda}_{z}\rangle\\
&+2i\theta\langle k^{2}B^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}},B^{\Lambda}_{z}\rangle\Big)\bar{u}^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime})q(z^{\prime},x)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}\int dzdz^{\prime}q(z^{\prime},z)(-i\partial_{\theta}V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(z-z^{\prime})u^{\theta}_{t}(z)\bar{u}^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime}))

(5.34)

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{Re}\langle G_{0}^{\Lambda},B_{0}^{\Lambda}\rangle-2\theta\mathrm{Re}\langle\omega B^{\Lambda}_{0},B^{\Lambda}_{0}\rangle+\partial_{\theta}\tfrac{1}{2}\langle u,V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}\ast|u|^{2}u\rangle,

where we used that
|u^{\theta}_{t}|^{2}
is independent of
\theta
and
integrates to one.

Mixed terms in
a,a^{*}
,

b,b^{*}
(
5.26c
)
. Mixed terms with, say,
a^{*}_{k}b^{*}_{x}

arise from the derivatives of the respective terms in
\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}
,
\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}
, and from the commutator

[\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\theta},\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}]
if one commutes a term
with one
a^{\#}
and one
b^{\#}
with terms with two
a^{\#}
s or two
b^{\#}
s.
The commutator

\displaystyle\bigg[

\displaystyle\int dkdl\Big(2M^{\Lambda}(k,-l)a^{*}_{k}a^{*}_{l}+M^{\Lambda}(k,l)a^{*}_{k}a^{*}_{l}+\text{h.c.}\Big),

(5.35)

\displaystyle\int dxdm\,\Big(\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(m,x)a^{*}_{m}b^{*}_{x}-\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(-m,x)a_{m}b^{*}_{x}\Big)+\text{h.c}\bigg]

vanishes identically, as one easily checks.

We group the remaining terms into two parts,
X=X^{(1)}+X^{(2)}
, which
vanish separately. Spelling things out, we set

\displaystyle X^{(1)}(k,x)

\displaystyle=(i\partial_{\theta}-\tau_{t}(x))(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)u^{\theta}_{t})(x)+(-i\partial_{t}+h_{\theta}+\omega(k))\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,x),

\displaystyle X^{(2)}(k,x)

\displaystyle=\int dy\Big(K_{\theta}^{(1),\Lambda}(x,y)\kappa_{t}^{\Lambda}(k,y)+K_{\theta}^{(2),\Lambda}(x,y)\overline{\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(-k,y)}\Big),

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{(1)}(k,x)

\displaystyle=(i\partial_{\theta}-\tau_{t}(x))(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)^{*}u^{\theta}_{t})(x)+(i\partial_{t}-h_{\theta}+\omega(k))\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(-k,x),

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{(2)}(k,x)

\displaystyle=-X^{(2)}(-k,x).

To see that
X^{(1)}(k,x)=0
, we use (
5.29
) with

T_{\theta}=L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)
and the identity

(5.36)

\nabla\tau_{t}(x)=\nabla 2\mathrm{Re}\langle iB_{x},\alpha_{t}^{\theta}\rangle=2\mathrm{Re}\langle kB_{x},\alpha_{t}^{\theta}\rangle=F_{\alpha_{t}^{\theta}}(x)

to obtain

\displaystyle(i\partial_{\theta}-\tau_{t}(x))(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)u^{\theta}_{t})=q[L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k),\tau_{t}]u_{t}^{\theta}+q(i\partial_{\theta}L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k))u_{t}^{\theta}

\displaystyle\quad=q\Big(2\theta B^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)k[-i\nabla,\tau_{t}]-iG^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)+2iB^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)k(-i\nabla+2\theta F_{\alpha_{t}^{\theta}})\Big)u^{\theta}_{t}

(5.37)

\displaystyle\quad=q\Big(-iG^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)+2B^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)k\nabla+2i\theta B^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)kF_{\alpha_{t}^{\theta}}\Big)u^{\theta}_{t}.

Using

\displaystyle[-\Delta,B_{x}^{\Lambda}(k)]

\displaystyle=k^{2}B_{x}^{\Lambda}(k)+2kB_{x}^{\Lambda}(k)i\nabla

\displaystyle[A_{\alpha},B^{\Lambda}_{x}(k)]

\displaystyle=[2(-i\nabla)\langle kB_{x},\alpha\rangle+\text{h.c},B^{\Lambda}_{x}(k)]=-2kB^{\Lambda}_{x}(k)\underbrace{2\mathrm{Re}\langle kB_{x},\alpha\rangle}_{=F_{\alpha}(x)}

we find in the same way

\displaystyle(-i\partial_{t}+h_{\theta}+\omega(k))\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,x)

\displaystyle=q\Big(i\omega(k)B^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}+[-\Delta+\theta A_{\alpha_{t}^{\theta}},iB^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)]\Big)u^{\theta}_{t}

(5.38)

\displaystyle=q\Big(iG^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}-2B^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)k\nabla-2i\theta B^{\Lambda}_{(\cdot)}(k)kF_{\alpha_{t}^{\theta}}\Big)u^{\theta}_{t}.

This equals the negative of (
5.37
), so
X^{(1)}\equiv 0
.

The equality
X^{(2)}\equiv 0
follows simply by expanding the expressions:

\displaystyle\int dy\,K_{\theta}^{(2),\Lambda}(x,y)\overline{\kappa_{t}^{\Lambda}(-k,y)}

\displaystyle=\int dydzdz^{\prime}dz^{\prime\prime}\,q(x,z)q(y,z^{\prime})u^{\theta}_{t}(z)u^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime})V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(z-z^{\prime})\overline{iB^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime\prime}}(-k)u^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime\prime})q(y,z^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle=\int dzdz^{\prime}dz^{\prime\prime}\,q(x,z)q(z^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime})u^{\theta}_{t}(z)u^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime})V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(z-z^{\prime})\bar{u}^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime\prime})(-iB^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime\prime}}(k))

\displaystyle=-\int dzdz^{\prime}\,q(x,z)u^{\theta}_{t}(z)|u^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime})|^{2}V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(z-z^{\prime})iB^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}}(k)

\displaystyle\quad+i\int dz\,q(x,z)(V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}\ast|u^{\theta}_{t}|^{2})(z)\langle u^{\theta}_{t},B^{\Lambda}_{\cdot}(k)u^{\theta}_{t}\rangle

(5.39)

\displaystyle=-\int dy\,K_{\theta}^{(1),\Lambda}(x,y)\kappa_{t}^{\Lambda}(k,y),

where the last equality is obtained by performing the same calculation for

K^{(1)}\kappa
, which just changes the location of some complex
conjugates. This implies vanishing of
\widetilde{X}^{(2)}
and the
argument for
\widetilde{X}^{(1)}
is completely analogous to that for

X^{(1)}
.

Terms quadratic in

a,a^{*}
(
5.26d
)
. The only way to obtain a term with
two
a^{\#}
s from the commutator
[\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\theta},\mathbb{D}_{t}^{\Lambda}]
is to commute two terms with an

a^{\#}
and one
b^{\#}
each. Since the coefficient

M^{\Lambda}(k,l)
(
5.11b
) of the terms with two
a^{\#}
s in

\mathbb{H}^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(t)
is independent of
\theta
, we obtain for
the coefficients in (
5.26d
)

(5.40a)

\displaystyle A(k,l)

\displaystyle=-4i\theta M^{\Lambda}(k,-l)

\displaystyle\quad-\int dx\Big((qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)u^{\theta}_{t})(x)\overline{\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(l,x)}+\overline{(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)^{*}u^{\theta}_{t})(x)}\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(-l,x)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\int dx\Big(\overline{(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(l)u^{\theta}_{t})(x)}\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,x)+(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(l)^{*}u^{\theta}_{t})(x)\overline{\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(-k,x)}\Big)

(5.40b)

\displaystyle E_{a}

\displaystyle=-i\int dkdx\Big(\overline{(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)u^{\theta}_{t})(x)}\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(k,x)+(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)^{*}u^{\theta}_{t})(x)\overline{\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(-k,x)}\Big)

(5.40c)

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}(k,l)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} 2i\theta M^{\Lambda}(k,l)+\int dx\,\Big(&\overline{(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)^{*}u^{\theta}_{t})(x)}\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(l,x)\\
&+(qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)u^{\theta}_{t})(x)\overline{\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(-l,x)}\Big).\end{aligned}

To see that
A(k,l)=0
, we first calculate using that

q^{2}=q=1-|u^{\theta}_{t}\rangle\langle u^{\theta}_{t}|
and

\displaystyle\int dx\Big((qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)u^{\theta}_{t})(x)\overline{\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(l,x)}+\overline{qL^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)^{*}u^{\theta}_{t}(x)}\kappa^{\Lambda}_{t}(-l,x)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=\begin{aligned} \int dzdz^{\prime}\,q(z^{\prime},z)\Big(&(L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)u^{\theta}_{t})(z)(-iB^{\Lambda}_{z^{\prime}}(-l))\bar{u}^{\theta}_{t}(z^{\prime})\\
&+\overline{(L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k)^{*}u^{\theta}_{t})(z^{\prime})}iB^{\Lambda}_{z}(-l)u^{\theta}_{t}(z)\Big)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad=\langle u^{\theta}_{t},i[L^{\Lambda}_{\theta}(k),B_{(\cdot)}^{\Lambda}(-l)]u^{\theta}_{t}\rangle

(5.41)

\displaystyle\quad=2i\theta\langle u^{\theta}_{t},B_{(\cdot)}^{\Lambda}(-l)lkB_{(\cdot)}^{\Lambda}(k)u_{t}^{\theta}\rangle=-2i\theta M^{\Lambda}(k,-l).

The second line in (
5.40a
) is the complex conjugate
of this with
k,l
exchanged, so it equals
2i\theta M^{\Lambda}(k,-l)
.
This implies that
A\equiv 0
. The argument for
\widetilde{A}\equiv 0
is
essentially the same.

It remains to evaluate
E_{a}
. We have by the calculation of
A(k,l)

(5.42)

\displaystyle E_{a}=2\theta\int dkM^{\Lambda}(k,-k)=-2\theta\langle k^{2}B_{0}^{\Lambda},B_{0}^{\Lambda}\rangle.

Consequently with (
5.34
)

\displaystyle E_{a}+E_{b}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{Re}\langle G_{0}^{\Lambda},B_{0}^{\Lambda}\rangle-2\theta\mathrm{Re}\langle(k^{2}+\omega)B^{\Lambda}_{0},B^{\Lambda}_{0}\rangle+\partial_{\theta}\tfrac{1}{2}\langle u,V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}\ast|u|^{2}u\rangle

\displaystyle=(2-2\theta)\mathrm{Re}\langle G_{0}^{\Lambda},B_{0}^{\Lambda}\rangle+\partial_{\theta}\tfrac{1}{2}\langle u,V^{\Lambda}_{\theta}\ast|u|^{2}u\rangle

(5.43)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}E^{\Lambda}_{\theta}.

This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

6.
Estimates for the
generators

In this section we establish the inequalities on the different generators of
the dynamics considered in the previous sections. This includes the
generators of the fluctuation dynamics for
e^{-itH_{N}^{\mathrm{D}}}
and its
Bogoliubov approximation used in the proofs of Theorems

3.2
and

4.2
, given in
Sections
6.4
,
6.3
,
respectively. Similar bounds for the generators associated with the dressing
flow and its Bogoliubov approximation are given in
Section
6.5
.

6.1.
Fock space operator bounds

We start by proving a general bound for operators on Fock space that will
prove very useful.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
n_{a},n_{b},m_{a},m_{b}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
M=n_{a}+n_{b}+m_{a}+m_{b}
,
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
and

T:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\otimes m_{b}}\otimes\mathfrak{h}_{t}^{\otimes m_{a}}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\otimes n_{b}}\otimes\mathfrak{h}_{-s}^{\otimes n_{a}}

be a bounded operator of norm
\tau
with an integral kernel

T((K,X),(L,Y))\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3M})
. Set

A_{n}(K)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{k_{i}}\text{ and }B_{n}(X)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{x_{i}}.

Then for all
0\leq r_{b}\leq n_{b}+m_{b}
,
0\leq r_{a}\leq n_{a}+m_{a}
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\Big\langle\chi,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3M}}T(X,K,Y,L)B^{*}_{n_{b}}(X)A^{*}_{n_{a}}(K)A_{m_{a}}(L)B_{m_{b}}(Y)dXdKdYdL\xi\Big\rangle\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\tau\|(\mathcal{N}_{b}+M)^{\frac{n_{b}+m_{b}-r_{b}}{2}}(\mathcal{N}_{a}+M+1)^{r_{a}}\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{2s})^{\frac{n_{a}}{2}}\chi\|

\displaystyle\qquad\times\|(\mathcal{N}_{b}+M)^{\frac{r_{b}}{2}}(\mathcal{N}_{a}+1)^{-r_{a}}\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{2t})^{\frac{m_{a}}{2}}\xi\|.

Proof.

To keep the notation manageable we give the proof in the case
n_{b}=m_{b}=0
,
the generalization is straightforward.
Set
n=n_{a}
,
m=m_{a}
, and let
\chi\in D(\textnormal{d}\Gamma(\omega^{2s})^{n/2})
, then

\displaystyle\int\Big\|\prod_{i=1}^{n}\omega^{s}(k_{i})a_{k_{i}}\chi\Big\|^{2}dK

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\omega^{2s}(k_{i})\Big)\Big\langle\chi,a_{k_{1}}^{*}\cdots a_{k_{n}}^{*}a_{k_{n}}\cdots a_{k_{1}}\chi\Big\rangle dK

(6.1)

\displaystyle=\|\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{2s})^{n/2}\chi\|^{2},

so

(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\mapsto\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\omega^{s}(k_{i})a_{k_{i}}\Big)\chi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3n},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}),

and analogously for
\xi
. Hence

\displaystyle\bigg|\Big\langle\chi,\int T(K,L)A_{n}^{*}(K)A_{m}(L)dKdL\,\xi\Big\rangle\bigg|

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \bigg|\int dKdL\Big\langle(\mathcal{N}_{a}+1)^{r_{a}}&\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\omega^{s}(k_{i})a_{k_{i}}\Big)\chi,\\
&T(K,L)(\mathcal{N}_{a}+1)^{-r_{a}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\omega^{-s}(k_{i})\Big)A_{m}(L)\xi\Big\rangle\bigg|\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq\|\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{2s})^{\frac{n}{2}}(\mathcal{N}_{a}+n+1)^{r_{a}}\chi\|

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big\|\int\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\omega^{-s}(k_{i})\Big)T(K,L)A_{m}(L)(\mathcal{N}_{a}+m+1)^{-r_{a}}\xi dL\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3n}_{K},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F})}

(6.2)

\displaystyle\leq\|T\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{t}^{\otimes m}\to\mathfrak{h}_{-s}^{\otimes n}}\|\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{2s})^{\frac{n}{2}}(\mathcal{N}_{a}+M+1)^{r_{a}}\chi\|\|\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{2t})^{\frac{m}{2}}(\mathcal{N}_{a}+1)^{-r_{a}}\xi\|.

This proves the claim.
∎

Two special cases of the previous lemma we use frequently are given
separately below.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and

\chi,\xi\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}
, we have

(6.3a)

\displaystyle\big|\big\langle\chi,\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{s/2})\xi\big\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq\|\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{s})^{1/2}\chi\|\,\|\mathcal{N}_{a}^{1/2}\xi\|,

(6.3b)

\displaystyle\|\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{s/2})\chi\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{N}_{a}^{1/2}\textnormal{d}\Gamma_{a}(\omega^{s})^{1/2}\chi\|.

Proof.

The first inequality is a special case of Lemma
6.1
(
t=0
,

T\alpha=\omega^{s}\alpha
,
m_{a}=n_{a}=1
,
r_{a}=0
). The second inequality
follows from the first by taking the supremum over
\|\xi\|=1
.
∎

6.2.
Preliminary estimates

Here we provide some bounds on the terms appearing in the mean-field
Hamiltonians as well as the kernels of the Bogoliubov Hamiltonians. These
will later be combined with the operator bounds from the previous section to
prove the estimates for the generators.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
G
,
B
and
V
be defined as
in (
2.17
), (
2.18
) and
(
3.2b
). For every
s>0
there exists a constant
C>0

such that

(6.4a)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{V}\|_{L^{1+s}}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(6.4b)

\displaystyle\|kB_{0}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{-s}}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(6.4c)

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\quad\big\langle|k|^{n}B_{0},|\alpha|\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{(n-1)+s}},

(6.4d)

\displaystyle\forall n\in\{1,2,3\}\quad\|\langle k^{n}B_{(\cdot)},\alpha\rangle u\|_{{L^{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{(n-2)/2+s}}\|u\|_{H^{n/2}}

for all
u\in H^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}_{n-1+s}
.
Moreover, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
C>0
so that for
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}

(6.4e)

\displaystyle\|\langle G_{(\cdot)},\alpha\rangle u\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon(\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}}^{2})+C\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

In view of the formula for
V
, we have
(2\pi)^{3/2}\widehat{V}(k)=-4G_{0}(k)B_{0}(k)+2\omega(k)B_{0}^{2}(k)
, and the first three inequalities
then follow immediately from the integrability properties of

G_{0}(k)=\omega(k)^{-1/2}
and

B_{0}(k)=(k^{2}+\omega(k))^{-1}\omega(k)^{-1/2}
.

For (
6.4d
), we use the Fourier representation in
x

together with Parseval to write

(6.5)

\displaystyle\|\big\langle k^{n}B_{(\cdot)},\alpha\big\rangle u\|_{{L^{2}}}^{2}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\Big\|\int dk\,k^{n}B_{0}(k)\alpha(k)\int dp\,e^{ip(\cdot)}\,\hat{u}(p-k)\Big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\int dk\,k^{n}B_{0}(k)\alpha(k)\,\hat{u}(\cdot-k)\Big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int dpdkd\ell\,\frac{k^{n}B_{0}(k)\overline{\alpha(k)}}{\omega(p-k)^{n/2}}\frac{\ell^{n}B_{0}(\ell)\alpha(\ell)}{\omega(p-\ell)^{n/2}}\omega(p-k)^{\frac{n}{2}}\widehat{u}(p-k)\omega(p-\ell)^{\frac{n}{2}}\overline{\widehat{u}(p-\ell)}.

Since
k\mapsto|k|^{n}B_{0}(k)^{2}\omega(k)^{2-2s}
and
k\mapsto\omega(k)^{-n}
are radial and decreasing functions for
s>0
and
n\in\{1,2,3\}
, it follows by symmetric rearrangement that

(6.6)

\displaystyle\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int dk\frac{|k|^{n}B_{0}(k)^{2}\omega(k)^{2-2s}}{\omega(p-k)^{n}}\leq\int dk\,B_{0}(k)^{2}\omega(k)^{2-2s}\leq C.

With Cauchy–Schwarz we thus find

(6.7)

\displaystyle\|\big\langle k^{n}B_{(\cdot)},\alpha\big\rangle u\|_{{L^{2}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int dpdkd\ell\,\frac{|k|^{n}B_{0}(k)^{2}\omega(k)^{2-2s}}{\omega(p-k)^{n}}|\ell|^{n}\omega(\ell)^{2s-2}|\alpha(\ell)|^{2}\omega(p-\ell)^{n}|\widehat{u}(p-\ell)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int d\ell\,\omega(\ell)^{n+2s-2}|\alpha(\ell)|^{2}\int dp\,(|p|^{2}+1)^{\frac{n}{2}}|\widehat{u}(p)|^{2}\leq C\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{n/2+s-1}}^{2}\|u\|_{H^{n/2}}^{2}.

The final inequality (
6.4e
) is proved in a similar way,
but to obtain the small constant in front of the energy norm we start by
introducing a cutoff
\Lambda<\infty
. We then bound

(6.8)

\|\langle G_{(\cdot)},\alpha\rangle u\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\langle\mathds{1}_{|\cdot|>\Lambda}G_{(\cdot)},\alpha\rangle u\|_{L^{2}}+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\|\mathds{1}_{|\cdot|\leq\Lambda}G_{0}\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}_{-1/2}}\|u\|_{L^{2}}.

Now the first term is treated exactly as for (
6.4d
),
which yields

(6.9)

\displaystyle\|\langle\mathds{1}_{|\cdot|>\Lambda}G_{(\cdot)},\alpha\rangle u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|\alpha\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}}^{2}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\int_{|k|>\Lambda}dk\frac{|G_{0}(k)|^{2}}{\omega(k)^{3}}.

Choosing
\Lambda
so that the final integral is less or equal to

\varepsilon^{2}
proves the claim.
∎

The next lemma collects bounds for the different potentials that appear in
the mean-field equations introduced in Section

3.2
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
V
be defined
by (
3.2b
) and
f_{u}
by (
3.4e
). There exists
a constant
C>0
such that for all
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with

\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1

(6.10a)

\displaystyle\|V\ast|u|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|V^{2}\ast|u|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla(V\ast|u|^{2})\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{3/2},

(6.10b)

\displaystyle\|f_{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|f_{u}\|_{\mathfrak{h}_{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

Moreover, for every
s>0
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all

u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\|u\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
